INTRODUCTION

EED we be surprised if Ganeda, God of Succesa, achieved for himuelf a truly
Nyemeneute career? As the reader will gather from the present work, his striking
figure won all the Eastern countries in turn. His presentation to the European
and American publics was all that was required to make him known throughout
the world, and every one will agree that for the last step he could not have chowen
better sponsors than the author of The Goda of Northern Buddhism and the Clarendon
Presa.

Blinking his cunning little eyes on either side of his huge trunk, he can boast,
with a certain feeling of gratification, that in spite of his uncouth looks, or perhaps
because of them, he is now universally accepted; and this must be all the more
flattering to his self-esteem, since as he himself is quite aware—his humble origins
are evident to every one. A mere glance at him shows that he still beara unquestion-
able signs of his modest lineage. Even if we ignore for the moment his ungainly
elephant’s head, yet his short arms, the bulging girth overhanging his stunted legs,
are enough to place him at once in the class to which he belongs. Obviously, Ganeéa
is linked with those stout, thickset goblins with which the earliest sculptures of
ancient India have made us familiar, and that appear so often in the texts, now as
the imps of Mara, the Buddhist Satan, now aa the yakeas of Kubera, God of Wealth,
now as the rikgasas of Kubera’s brother, Ravana, and sometimes as the ganas ofthe
‘King of the Mountains’, Siva. For sheer antiquity their race can hold ite own.
Those grotesque dwarfs spring from a family which can be traced, since time began,
from one end of the old continent to the other. By their misshapen bodies, their
guardianship of treasure hoards, and by their freakish and too often evil characters,
the gnomes of India (known under the various names already mentioned and many
more besides, such as kumbhanda, pisdca, vetdla, &c.) are unquestionably the cousins,
morally and physically, of the Scandinavian trolls, the Celtic korrigans, the Anglo-
Saxon goblins, the German kobolds, the Thraco-Phrygian Kabiri, not to mention
the Arabian jinn. Even to-day we find them doeply rooted in our countryside,
diaporting themselves on moora, in woods, and in caves; but venerable die-hards as
they are, those spirits occupy the lowest rung in the hierarchic ladder. Ganeéa,
as his name implies, rose to the rank of chieftain (iéa) ; yet in attaining the dignity
of ‘Lord of the Hordes’, his appearance remained like that of his followers, the
ganas.

In this respect he was not so fortunate as the other ‘army-leaders’ who form the
‘General Staff’ of Indian genii. ‘The artist is only too willing to give them a pleasing
form, as in the case of Field-Marshal (mahd-send-pati} Skanda. It is rare that he will
ever do more than spoil this elegance by adding plumpness, necessarily associated
in the Indian mind with wealth, as, for example, in the representation of General
(send-pats) Paficika, What, however, differentiates Ganeda from his fellow-spirita
is not only his excesaive corpulence, but a still greater handicap, equally impossible
to conceal—his elephant’s head: nothing more ia needed to suggest, in fact to prove,

--- Page 14 ---
xvi INTRODUCTION

his animal origin. In the pantheons of Groece or Egypt, of Mesopotamia or India,
deities with heads of animals or birds are not wanting, and it would seem as if there
were some fixed law of iconography in regard to them. As far as we know, no human-
shaped deity, even in the case of a metamorphosis, thinks of adopting an animal
mask otherwise than en passant; whereas all the theriomorphic gods, on the con-
trary, show a tendency to shed their original bodies of lions, monkeys, jackals,
ibises, falcons, &c., and permanently to assume human form. During the period of
transformation, morphological compromises of all kinds were imagined by artists
and by devotees, from goat-hoofed genii to deities with horns. But as a general rule,
the part of the animal body which the god or goddess in process of anthropomor-
phosis clings to Jongest is the head, just as the piece of national dress that Asiatic
peoples in the throes of Europeanization relinquish last ia the head-dress, On che
other hand, this very conservatism, so easy to understand, betrays the preceding
stages of a transformation still incomplete, Thus, when dealing with a therianthropo-
morphic figure of Ganesa’s type, we can easily trace it back to the animal prototype
from which it came; and here we plunge into the oldest layer of superstition which
our developed minds can grasp: totem worship and agrarian rites, Ts it surprising,
then, that Ganesa’s biography, thrusting its roots into the most obscure beliefs of
the human race, should be difficult to write ?

One guiding ray, however, seems to pierce the darkness surrounding him and his
origin, An objective analysis of the figure of Ganesa and the convincing analogy
provided by every ancient form of religion lead us to admit that he made his entry
into the world of things sacred under the guise of an elephant. Now while the
existence of goblins is a matter for personal belief or disbelief, that of the elephant
is an obvious fact. Moreover this odd creature was bound, through its great height,
unequalled strength, and surprising docility, through the extraordinary dexterity
of ita trunk as well as its intelligence, wrongly or rightly praised, to hold a lange
place in Indian folklore. Yet these data only add to our sense of frustration, since,
Curious as it may seem, we are unable eo far to discover any relation between the
worship offered to Ganeda and the surviving manifestations of old popular super-
stitions, such as, for instance, the peculiar regard in which the white elephant is held
in those parta of Indo-China where Indian influence once prevailed. We are not
more euccessful if we search for clues in the innumerable puns and comparisons
which abound in Sanskrit literature concerning the protuberances above ita fore-
head and the honied liquor that oozes from its temples during tho mating season.
Nor are the legends more helpful than the poems. In several of the jatakas or tales
of the past lives of the Buddha, tho elephant plays an important part, his role being
inspired by a whole range of emotional contrasts, from the fury of the savage
solitaire destroying everything in his path to the sublime generosity of the dying
King of the Herd, who forgives his murderer, the hunter, and bestows his ivory
tusks upon him. During the last existence of the Buddha the elephant reappears
frequently: the future Sakya-muni is said to have come down from heaven in the

form of a young elephant; and when he retired into solitude, he was waited upon
by an elephant, while one of his most important miracles was the taming of a mad

--- Page 15 ---
INTRODUCTION xvii
elephant that his enemies let loose against him in the main thoroughfare of Raja-
griha. Herc again though, in whatever light we examine these edifying stories, we
find no means of linking them up with the little that we know of the personality and
cult of Ganega; and our efforts are equally fruitless when we turn to the Brahmanic
mythology; for we see no connexion whatever either between our hero and the
famous mount of Indra, the elephant Airavata, or between him and the eight cosmic
elephants which, facing the four cardinal points and the four intermediary regions,
are believed to support the earth on their backs. It is true that on either side of
the bay of Bengal, in the Buddhist as well as the Brahmanic texts, we are dealing
with Indian ideas; but even if those beliefa, metaphors, and legends have sprung up
from the same soil, it is none the less clear that they do not follow the same trail nor
develop on the same plane.

The almost complete absence of ancient documents relating to Ganega is eome-
what disconcerting, for it is evident that without them no historical reconstruction
is possible, Yet must one necessarily succumb to the habitual failing of the philo-
logist whose learned candour leads him to infer that all things have their beginning
from the time of their first mention in the texts ? Such a conclusion would be absurd,
for in any given place and moment there are always many more social facta than
those recorded in writing. It is obviously true even of our own days, and the farther
we delve into the illiterate past of humanity the more we understand this to be the
case. One must be as short-sighted as a bookworm to deduce from the lack of any
mention of Ganeéga in the early epics, that the god did not exist before the fourth or
fifth century of our era, and it would require a certain amount of naiveté to believe
that this divinity could have suddenly been conceived ex nihtle, and his worship
established throughout the whole of India at such a comparatively recent date.
Whoever is experienced in the waye of that country knows well that by then the
Elephant-god had already behind him century upon century, if not thousands of
years, of silent waiting at the foot of the sacred tree of the pre-Aryan village, stand-
ing on the roughly built platform which he shared with the other rustic deities: with
the snakes which were to give birth to many Naga-kings, with the monkey which
was to become Hanuman, with the vulture which would later grow into Garuda,
with the lion, the boar, the tortoise, and the fish, all of which were in time to be
looked upon as avatars of Vignu. For those more or leaa shapeless stones, those
roughly carved pieces of wood or summarily outlined figures in clay, all those totems
or fetishes—once bathed in the blood of human victims and to-day still emeared
with minium-—were so deeply rooted in an unfathomable past that the aristocracy
of the new gods, introduced by the immigrant Aryans, were compelled to adopt
them in some form or other. Had they not come to terms with them they would
soon have found themselves estranged from the worship of the populace, and their
pricsts deprived of gifts and followers. It is probable that the Brahmine, the chosen

interpreters of the Divine Will, submitted to this but reluctantly; yet, as Manu
himself must admit, ‘in all things, necessity conetitutes law’.
Shall we now try to conjecture retrospectively what underlying idea or procise
- °

--- Page 16 ---
=x INTRODUCTION

frieze, a seventh goblin is represented with broad clephant’s ears on each side of his
human face, just like those little Atlantes discovered at Jaulian near Taxila, right
at the other end of India. For the time being we must wait as late as the fifth century
of our era to meet, in the temple of Bhumara in Central India, the first complete
realization of Ganeda’s type: the panels of its walls are decorated with numerous
ganas having either the head of a man or that of a lion, a boar, a monkey, or a
parrot; while the ruins of the central cella contained the oldest known representa-
tion of Ganega at last deified (Pl. 34). It may be interesting to note that this idol,
like the two mentioned above, has only one pair of arms; according to Professor
J. Ph. Vogel, this is also the case with a Gupta statue from Bhitari noticed by General
Cunningham. However, a second image of Ganesa, enshrined in one of the blank
dormer-windows at Bhumara, has four arms, It is the same with the one discovered
at Bhitargaon, where the god is depicted on the terra-cotta panels of the temple in
an attitude which is not absolutely hieratic, for he seems to be defending his plate
of cakes from the unwelcome attentions of a genius in human form- probably his
brother Skanda.

Such appear to us, up to the present, to be the débuts of the iconography of Ganeéga.
The gropings of his early representations reflect his first hesitating steps in the world
of divinities of highest rank where the texts will henceforth enable us to follow him.
Indeed, the creation of so curiously blended a figure, a stroke of genius on the part
of the sculptor, was also a stroke of luck for his model: we have no doubt that the
image itself contributed largely to the fame of the god. Yet we must not forget that
originally Ganeéa waa a kind of demon, or, to put it more politely, a jungle genius,
That he should become included in the retinue of the ‘Great Lord’ who, followed
by innumerable spirits more or less malignant, was believed to haunt the mountains
and forests, was an easy step, and one that seemed natural for him to take, since it
is written that.‘ while Vignu is the God of the Gods, Siva is the God of the Demons’.
To rise in rank until he became commander of those hordes and thus to enter the
inner circle of the cortege of Siva was a justifiable promotion, whether due to
seniority or to choice. He might then have remained in this capacity attached to
the God and his Goddess, side by side with the bull of the one and the tiger of the
other, on the same footing as Timbaru (or Tumbury), king of the Gandharvas and
conductor of the divine orchestra, whose horse’s head is still found in modern minia-
tures... had his devotees been satisfied with thisadvancement; but the latter aspired
to raise him up to the same level as Skanda, son of Siva, and thus set themselves
a difficult task. The purdnas faithfully reflect the perplexity in which his composite
figure involved them. It will be realized below (p. 6 et segg.) how Inborious were their
efforts to provide him with a divine ancestry, when it was alternatively claimed that
he was begotten by Siva alone, or by Devi alone; and these legends, depriving him
either of a father or of a mother, once admitted, there still remained the awkward
question of his elephant’s head. How was his divine birth to be reconciled with the
abnormal coupling in his body of man and beast? The least that could be done was
to declare that this peculiarity was accidental not congenital ; and from this again
wa8 born another geries of myths attempting to give a seemly, if not satisfactory,

--- Page 17 ---
INTRODUCTION xxi
explanation of an anomaly which might be pardonable in a rustic environment, but
was scarcely in keeping with the Olympian summits of Mount Kailaéa. The dis-
crepancies of those tales, even more than their inconsistency, would be enough to
prove that they had been invented as an afterthought for the needa of the cause and
by leas than indifferent scholara. This, however, was of no consequence; for no
sooner was Ganeéa established on the steps of the throne, so to speak, than the
G&napatya rect sought to promote him to the supreme rank, and make him usurp
the sovereign power, ousting in his ‘Conquest of the Cardina! Pointe’ (dig-vizaya)
even his putative father Siva.

As our task consisted in leading Ganeda to the threshold of his august career, we
shall leave him now that he is about to enter into the full light of art and literature.
Up to what point it is already possible to sketch the broad lines of his proto-history
is for the reader to judge. In the penumbra, becoming more obscure as one gropes
farther and farther back into the past, we have tried to discern, thanks to some
analogies and traditions, the first stage of Ganeéa's embryonic existence when he
was etill the ungainly, but already sacred, image of a genius-elephant. After that
long and dim period, comparable in a way to the larva stage in insect life, we then
attempted to follow him through his next development which, mysterious as the
traneformation of the chrysalis, ended in the ultimate type of a genius with the
head of an elephant. Undoubtedly the progress of research work and new archaeo-
logical discoveries cannot fail to add much, in time, to the frame-work of the present
brief analysie, and will lead in a more secure manner to the third and last period,
the one described below---the glorious éclat of which, it would be useless to try to
deny, is already on the decline owing to the profound changes now taking place in
the East. But to our way of thinking, what really matters ia the light which Gancéa’s
ascension throws on the religious evolution of India. It supplies a typical instance
of those devotions, in appearance new, in fact extremely old, which one usually
groups together under the appellation of Tantrism from the name of the books (tantra)
in which they are exposed at great length; a most conveniont way of designating
them, the evasiveness of the term being an excellent reason for avoiding a more
precise definition.

When one consults Indian echolars in regard to Tantrism, one would gather from
their writings that it was a kind of leprosy which broke out suddenly during the fifth
or sixth century of our era, rapidly contaminating the texts as well as the pantheons
of the various sects, whether Brabmanic or Buddhist. So it might seem indeed
at first glance; but, as Ganega’s concrete example shows, the evil was far deeper and
dated much farther back. A vast uprising, a veritable religious revolution, brought
forth through the intermediary of the sculptor’s chisel and the pandit’s calamus a
whole set of doctrines and a cohort of deities, just aa disquieting the one as the other,
that until then had lain hidden and almost unsuapected by archaeologists as well
aa by philologista. From the depths of India sprang forth those unrestrained magical
and spiritualistic divagations as well aa those abnormal or even ghastly idols, bring-
ing with them to the surface all the dregs of the old orgiastic rites at the same time

--- Page 18 ---
axii INTRODUCTION
as the whole rabble of evil spirite—thus provoking among the historians a not un-
justifiable outcry against such a moral, intellectual, and artistic regression. Only
it a0 happened that the tidal wave, meeting the thousand ramifications of the pro-
viously established secta, simply brought to each particular channel a new rush of
religious fervour. Ite rapid division into 60 many branches was the reason why the
fundamental breadth, unity, and antiquity of its overtiow became scarcely discern-
able. It is nevertheless responsible for what was achieved by India during the
medieval period and down to modern times in the way of religious literature and
iconography. On the other hand, if one sought to claim for it patents of nobility
in the early Indian past, it would not be difficult to recognize the precursory symp-
toms of thie movement in the passages of the Atharva-veda which were not entirely
disguised under a Brahmanic garb, thus reverting without effort to the pre-Aryan
epoch. In short, if we take Ganeda as a witness, Tantrism was not a contagious
malady which, at a comparatively recent date, suddenly attacked Brahmaniem,
Buddhism, Jainism, and so on; but like them it was a distinct religion, and what is
more, by far the oldest and the most widely epread in the peninsula. No wonder
that it finally absorbed all the others, forming now the basis as well as the link for
the extraordinary medley classified under the general label of Hinduism.

If Ganeda’s case helps us to understand the true nature of Tantrism, the new con-
ception thus gained about the latter gives us, in its turn, the possibility of placing his
biography more accurately in its real onvironment; and the astonishing good fortune
of the Elephant-headed god immediately loses much of the exceptional character
that we might otherwise have felt inclined to give it, his advent becoming only one
element of the general reappearance of old superstitions and beliefe. These had
remained inveterate but latent, since they were refused the double means of expres-
aion and propagands provided by literature and plastic art. It was largely due to
the ever increasing diffusion of the latter that, in tho midst of the severe social com-
motions brought about by the barbaric invasions, they succeeded in getting the
upper hand and revealed themselves as completely as they did. Like so many of hia
kind, fairies or genii, Ganeéa, although more fortunate, was merely carried along on
the tide of a great upward movement, analogous to that which, since all time, has

urged the caates of India to rise to a superior level, yet with the important difference
that the lower grades of deities have reached their goal far more rapidly than mere
humanity: therefore we ventured to call their ascent a revolution.

This may require an explanation: that a revolution suddenly and inevitably
carries the lower strata of the people to the top is undeniable; but it is not unusual
for those who have benefited by the upheaval to try to adopt the manner and style
of those whom they have supplanted ; and Gareéa, the divine Opportuniat, did not
escape from the common law. If it ia true that certain among his representationa,
by their unseemly or gruesome character, still betray some regrettable remains of
vulgarity, the ex-Demon of the Jungle assumed more and moro the aspect of a bon
bourgeois, We soon find his corpulent figure at the entrance of temples, opposite
to that of Kubera (otherwise Maha-kals or Jambhala), the Dispenser of Riches.
Except for their faces, the two gods resemble each other like twin brothers; and

--- Page 19 ---
INTRODUCTION xxiij
to the worshippers passing to and fro, the one murmurs: ‘May you be rich!’ while
the other whispers in their ear: ‘May you be successful in your enterprises!’ What
could be more reassuring to well-to-do people? Let us mention, by the way, that
Ganeéa’s rat, upon which the mythologista have built such far-fetched theories (p. 1),
is simply the counterpart of the mongoose of Kubera; as we know with certainty
that the mongoose symbolizes a well-lined purse, it is more than probable that the
rat evokes the well-filled granary where it is always an assiduous guest, Indeed
we meet him again just as busily engaged near the bales of rice on which the merry
Dai-Koku, the Mahakdla of the Japanese, is crouching.

In short, the aspect under which the ‘Prince of Obstacles’ likes to reveal himself
is, aa a rule, wholly benevolent. The great Indian scholar, to whom it would have
fallen to write this Preface had not his sudden death left it for us to fulfil hia promise,
summed up perfectly the impression that the images of the Elephant-faced God make
upon us, when he wrote (Sylvain Lévi, Le Népal, 1, pp. 383-4), that Ganesa with
his accessories forms a most captivating whole: ‘His atrange but good-natured
physiognomy arrests our attontion as well as our sympathy '—an opinion which the
following pages will prove to be true.

SRI GANESAYA NAMAH
CHAPTER I
INDIA: ORIGIN OF GANESA. GANESA IN INDIAN
LITERATURE

Gare. Lord of the Ganas,! although among the latest deities to be admitted

to the Brahmanic pantheon, waa, and still is, the most universally adored of all
the Hindu gods, and his image is found in practically every part of India. The
popularity of Ganega extended to Nepal and Chinese Turkestan and croased the
seas to Java, Bali, and Borneo, while his worship was not unknown in Tibet, Burma,
Siam, China, Indo-China, and Japan.

Certain authorities believe that Ganega was originally a Dravidian deity wor-
shipped by the aboriginal populations of India who were sun-worshippers ; and that
Ganeéa on hia wihana, the rat, symbolized a sun-god? overcoming the animal, which,
in ancient mythology, was a symbol of the night.$

Others are of the opinion that his elephant-head and bis mount, the rat, indicate
that, although he may have been taken over from indigenous mythology, he belonged
originally to an animal cult.* This veems a plausible theory, since his image is found
in Hindu temples worshipped in company with the animal avatare of Vianu.

It is not known whether Ganesa is to be looked upon as an original or a derivative
deity ; but it is probable that he was primarily the totem of a Dravidian tribe. The
primitive effigies were often animal-headed; and the elephant, being the largeat
animal in India as well as the shrewdest, would assuredly have figured among them.

We do not know what was his ancient Dravidian epithet ; but if we judge from
the earliest sanskrit name that is known to us, Ekadanta, be was probably designated
as He of One Tusk. The Dravidian worda pajlu and pella both signify tooth, tusk
of the elephant.® It therefore seems possible that his Tamil name, Pillaiyar, is a
corruption of his ancient Dravidian title although, in ita present form, there is no
meaning of ‘tusk’. Pie is the Tamil word for child and Pillaiyar means ‘noble
child’; but Bagchi is of the opinion that pifle originally meant the ‘young of the
elephant’, for the Pali word pillaka haa the significance of ‘a young elephant’.®

In a collection of Vedic mantras in the Taittiriya-dranyaka,’ there is a mystic
prayer addressed to a god, Dantin, He of the Tusk (danta), which secms to refer to
the Elephant-faced god, for the mantra comes in a suite of mantras addressed to two

deities Karttikeya and Nandi, the bull, with whom Ganesa is often associated not
only in the purdnas but in the most ancient Indian sculptures of the deity. Besides
which, the commentator, Sdyana, apparently indicates an elephant-faced god when
he explains that he was possessed of a tunda (nose, beak, trunk) which was mkra
(twisted, curved); and that he carried a pile of corn, a club, and a sugar cane, all of
which might be attributes of Ganega.! He was known later in puranic texts as Vakra-
tugda or ‘He of the Curved Trunk’, for the epithet is to be found in a list of twelve
names of Ganeéa mentioned in a Ganapati-stotra® for his special worship ; while in the
Mahénindna-tantra, he is addressed as Raktatunda or ‘He of the Red Trunk’.4

Although he is apparently referred to in the above Vedic text, he is not to be
found in the Assembly of Vedic deities, at least, not under any name that is known
to us. The reason for this may be that in ancient times he was possibly an unim-
portant village god, worshipped exclusively by the lower classes. In fact, a verse
attributed to the ancient version of the Laws of Manu‘ seems to indicate his lowly
origin, for it runs as follows: ‘Siva is the god of the Brahmans while Ganeéa is the
god of the #idras’,* who were the aboriginal population.

There is no ancient legend, that is, earlier than the purdnas, which explains why
he was possessed of only one tusk; but it seems to have had an important significance
since his title ‘Ekadanta’ has followed him down to the present day. The explana-
tion may possibly be found in his connexion, at a very early date, with the harvest
ceremonies; and his promotion, in time, to the important role of ‘Lord of the
Harvest’. The rites and ceremonies as well as the deities associated with the Harvest
Festival were full of symbolic meaning; and undoubtedly the form of the Ekadanta
was invested with mystic significance. It seema natural that the one tusk of the
Harvest Lord, which gave him his ancient name, should symbolically stand for
the most important implement of the harvest, the plough, eapecially as the word
ekadanta may be translated: ‘ono tusk’ or ‘plough-share’.?

As the Elephant-faced god seems only to have been known to the uneducated
classes up to the sixth century, it is not surprising to find that he does not figure
in the Epic pantheon of deities. Even the name of Ganeéa or Ganedvara (Lord of
the Ganas) which is found both in the north and south India recension of the
Mahdbhérata® may not be attributed to him until much later, for it is the title of
Siva, his reputed father in puranic myths; nor does the epithet Ganapati® found in
the Rig-Veda (1. 23. 1) refer to him but to the important Vedic deity Brahmanaapati,!®
‘father of the goda’. Certain Indian pandits, however, look upon Ganeda as 6

derivative of this Vedic deity. Przyluski, on the contrary, is of the opinion that
Siva and Ganeéa were originally one and the same god; that is, that although
Ganeés does not figure in the Mahabhdrata as distinet from Siva (Ganeévara), he was
none the leea an aspect of Siva and might therefore have been considered identical

GANESA IN INDIAN LITERATURE 3
with Rudra-Siva, even although he was introduced into the Indian pantheon as
Ganeéa, Lord of the Ganas.'

The ganas, attendante of Siva, were benevolent deities of nine classes dwelling
on Mount Kailaéa; and mentioned in the Mahdbhdrata in connexion with the ganas
was another group called vindyakas who, although malevolent, were not insensible
to propitiation. According to Bhandarkar, propitiatory rites and ceremonies in their
worship were set up before the Christian era.? In the Afdnavagrhya-sttra the Vina-
yakas are mentioned as a group of four gods, but in later texts they are referred to
as ‘one’ deity; and in the Atharvasiras-upanishad, Rudra is identified with one
Vinayeke. In the Smrti of Yajiiavalkya, the group of four vindyakas was addressed
as ‘one’ god, who was looked upon as the son of Ambik&.* Keith is of the opinion
that this deity is akin to the later god, Ganeéa. At any rate, puranic myth explains
that the group of four rindyakas was merged into one definite deity whom Rudra
(Siva) appointed ‘ Leader of the Ganas’ or Gana-pati; and Ganapats- Vindyaka,* who
may be considered the carliest form of Ganesa as known in the purdnas, was given
the role of creating difficulties and obstructions if not properly propitiated. Accord-
ing to Bhandarkar, the cult of Ganapati-Vinayaka may already have been set up
by the end of the sixth century.’ The earliest mention of the demon-god ia in the
Smrti of Yajiavalkya which was possibly written in the sixth century ;* but no
reference to Ganeda as an elephant-headed deity ia to be found until the eighth, when,
in the opening stanzas of the Malatinuidhava, he is described as having the face of
an elephant.”

Although the Elephant-faced god is not referred to in either of the texts of the
two Hindu Epics—the Mahabhdrata and the Rémayana—we find a Ganeda distinct
from Siva (although alao called Ganeddna) with god-like qualities in the introduction
to the north India recension of the Mahabharata.

He is referred to as scribe to the sage Vyasa, writing down with super-human
rapidity Vyéaa’s dictation of the Mahabharata. There is reterence to this legend in
the Balabharata, a poem written in the ninth century, where Valmiki, author of
the Raméayana, on meeting Vyasa, aska him how the Mahdbhdrata is progressing.
The sage replies that after severe austerities he has been able to secure Mahi-Ganeéa
as his scribe, &c.*

Winternitz believes that the legend waa known long before the ninth century and
was not inserted into the introduction to the uorth India revision of the Hindu
Epic until 150 years later.* In the Introduction to the south India recension of the

The Nepalese miniature’ is in the Pivigaldmdld, a tantric manuscript of the thir-

‘The second miniature is a Rajput painting of the seventeenth century, now in the

the worshipping Vy4sa. In the upper right-hand corner is Brahmi, at whose sugges-

still in vttarka-mudrd and the upper left holds the axe, it would seem that Gancéa
had finished his task of amanuensis and was presenting, with his normal left hand,
the completed text to Vyasa.

There is a divergence of opinion as to how Ganega, ‘Remover of Obstacles’,
gained this reputation of ‘Patron of Letters’.

Prabodh Chandra Bagchi’ suggests that Ganesa was associated with writing
because of a confusion in regard to the word ‘siddhi'. From very ancient times, the
Hindu alphabet was called ‘siddham’ and the enumeration of the alphabet began
with the word siddht. As one of the epithets of Ganeda is Siddhidata, ‘Giver of
Success’, he believes it to be probable that his association with the word gave rise
to the legends depicting him as a scribe.

Bhandarkar’ is of the opinion that his reputation for wisdom was bom of a con-
fusion between Ganeéa and the Vedic god of Wisdom, Brhaspati; while Rao® identi-
fies him with the celestial guru, Brhaspati, himself. It is interesting to note here
that Brhaspati, an important god in the Rig-Veda, is described as carrying the axe
or ‘golden hatchet’, an attribute particularly ascribed to Ganeda, and that he also
was referred wo as Ganapati.

Coomaraswamy”? attributea his reputation as Patron of Letters to the double

pious Hindu would risk publishing any work without asking his benign patronage.
The growing popularity of Ganega was not founded, however, on his title of
Protector of the Literati. Under the name of Ganapati, he became a most important
deity toward the tenth century, when a mystic and powerful sect, the Ganapatyas,
established his worship to the exclusion of every other Hindu god, even of his
reputed parents, Siva and Parvatt. Temples were erected to him alone, with hia
vahana, the rat, guarding the entrance as did Nandi, the bull, the templea dedicated
to Siva. The largest one built in honour of Ganeda waa a rock-cut temple near
Trichinopoly known as Ucchi-pillaiyar Kovil.*
The cult of the Ganapatyas, who bore as a distinguishing mark a red circle on the
forehead, was esoteric; and Ganeda, looked upon as a Supreme Deity, waa believed
to be superior to Brahma, Visnu, and Siva, as may be seen in the following Gana-
patya hyma used in his worship.”
Om Jam: Praise be to thee, Ganapati!
Thou art the visible Reality. ... Thou art the Creator, the Preserver, the Destroyer,

Thou art Brahma, thou art Visnu, thou art Rudra. Thou art superior to the

Orn, praise be to thee, Ganapati!
The word om, which precedes every Brahman mantra, is composed of a-u-m; a for
Frahm, u for Vignu, and m for Siva, It is symbolized by the sanskrit eign 9%. The
sign attached on the right is called ‘Ganega’s trunk’. Ganeda is thus associated
with Brahma, Vignu, and Siva, since no Tite, except those dedicated to Brahman,
may be performed without his invocation.

annoyance.

From here on, there are several versions of the legend regarding the creation of
Ganeéa as Vighnesvara,? ‘ Lord of Obstacles’.

(1) Siva, much perturbed by the unforeseen consequences of his decree, fell into
deep meditation, and while immersed in profound thought a great brilliance
emanated from his forehead* and there sprang into existence a wondrous being
endowed with all the qualities of Siva. When the goddess Uma (Parvati) aaw the
surpassingly beautiful youth whom Siva had created of his own will and without
her participation, she uttered the following powerful curse: ‘May thy head resemblo
that of an clephant and thy body be deformed by a huge belly.’

Siva seems to have accepted this curse as final, and without demur acknowledged
the elephant-headed youth as his son in the following terms: ‘Thy name shall be
Caneéa, Vinayaka, Vighnaraja, the son of Siva. Success and disappointment shall
proceed from thee. Thou shalt be worshipped and invoked before the other gads on all
occasions‘ or otherwise the object and prayer of him who omits to do so, shall fail.’S

The second veraion eliminates all participation of Siva in the creation of Ganeéa.

(2) Siva, after listening to the supplications of Indra and the minor gods, replied
that he was unable to grant their request, for baving given his promise to his faithful
followers worshipping at hia shrine of Somedvara, it could not be withdrawn. But,
he suggested, why not appeal to the goddess Parvati, his consort, who might find
some meana of extricating them from their present predicament ?

Indra then turned to the goddess Parvati, and kneeling before her with bowed
head and clasped hands, addressed her as follows: ‘Praise to thee, O, Supreme of
Goddesses. Supporter of the Universe. O Lotus-eyed. O Beloved of Siva. Grant
us thy aid and save us from this fearful dilemma.’*

Parvati, moved with compassion, brought into existence a ‘Creator of Obstacles’
(Vighneévara); but legends differ as to the mode of creation. The Skanda-purdna
has it that, after gently rubbing her body, Parvati produced a youth with four arms

and the head of an clephant. There is a curious version of the legend which runs
as follows:’ ‘Taking the unguents with which she anointed herself, Parvati mixed

Ganesa figured not only in the above puranic myths but in many mystic and magic
texts, both Brahmanic and Buddhist, from the fifth century onwards. Stotras were
offered in hia praise such as the hymn found in the Tandjur® where he is addressed
ag the great hero, conqueror of Mara, without equal, incomparable, great magician,
king of incantations, master of secret formulas. *To the Lord with the trunk of an
Elephant, homage!”

He was invoked in tantric sddhanas or mystic formulas for the invocation of a
deity when he was to be visualized with three eyes and many arms, and in forms
unknown in paintings or sculptures, such as in the Sarvadurgatipariéodhana,® where,

circles, Unfortunately most of the dhdranis in this tantra are not suitable for
publication. There are other dhdranis where he is referred to as being ignominioualy
trodden under foot by the goddesa Aparajita* or hy other gods or goddesses.
Sfantras, or short hymns of invocation believed to bave mystic or magic virtues,
were addressed to him ; and like the other Tantric gods, he was allotted a vija, that
is, a mystic ayllable by which he might be invoked. The Tantric vija of Ganeda was:

In spite of his apparent popularity in the Tantraa, he was, nevertheless, referred

‘Who else but thee, O Lord of the Three Worlds, is able to solve these doubts of
mine? Thou who knowest all’, Siva replies: ‘What is that thou aayest, O Great
Wise One and Beloved of my Heart? I will tell thee anything, be it ever so bound
in mystery, even that which should not be aaid before Ganega and Skanda. .. ."!
The author knows of no other text where Skanda is thus associated with his brother
Ganeéa, who, unlike Skanda, seema never to have been admitted to the Tantric
mysteries. In the Kula? rites of initiation, for instance, Gancéa was first supplicated
to remove all obstacles to the success of the initiation, after which he was invited
to depart by the following mantra of dismissal: ‘Lord of Obstacles, pardon me!'*;
and not until his auppoxed departure had taken place were the mystic rites of initia-
tion begun. It seems apparent from this that he was looked upon aa a god to be
feared until properly propitiated ; but the question arises as to why Ganeda, alone
of all the gods, should be excluded from the Tantric mysteries, and especially, from
the Kula rites of initiation, when the group of ‘Divine Mothers’, with whom, as
will be scen below, he was always associated, waa not only admitted to the mysteries,
but was worshipped several times during the Kula ceremonies. As a matter of fact,
very little is known of Ganeéa in the Tantra and not until more Tantric texts are
accessible for atudy will it be possible to determine the exact position of Ganeda in
relation to the other goda who were worshipped in the mysteries, or to find the key
to this puzzling Tantric aspect of the Elephant-faced god.

CHAPTER II
INDIA: GANESA IN ICONOGRAPHY FROM HINDU
TEXTS AND IMAGES
HERE is no trace of a Ganeéa cult in India before the fifth century a.v.,
Tathoweh aa we have seen above in the Vedic mantra addressed to the ‘One
with the Curved Trunk’ (Vakra-Tunda), a deity with the face of an elephant was
known long before the Gupta period. Nor is there reason to put faith in the tradi-
tion according to which his worship was carried from India to Nepal in the third
century u.c. by the daughter of the Buddhist king Asoka.) There would seem, how-
ever, to be conclusive evidence of his having been known under the epithet of
‘Ganega’ at least ag early as the beginning of our era, if the peculiar characters on
a Huvigka coin in the India Museum of Calcutta are, as Vincent Smith believed,
old Brahmi for ‘Ganeéga’.? The image on the coin, however, is that of Siva and the
epitbet may possibly refer to him ; but it may also illustrate Przyluski’s theory that
Siva and Ganeéa were originally one and the same deity. The name ‘Ganapati’ is
found as early aa the middle of the fourth century, in a Gupta inscription on the
Allahabad Pillar,® where the king Samudragupta mentions, among the kings that
he has ‘exterminated’, the king Ganapati Naga. This is the earliest instance of a
king being called ‘Ganapati’ and does not occur again until after the tenth century.
As thie name was particularly associated with Ganeéa,* may it therefore be inferred
that the god Ganapati was known as early as the fourth century? ‘here is sculp-
tural evidence of his popularity from the fifth century onwarda, although no inscrip-
tion has been found referring either to Ganeda or to Ganapati aa a god until after
the Gupta period.

It was possibly because of his early popularity as ‘Remover of Obstacles’ that
he waa attached to different groups of deities such aa the Navagrahas (the Nine
Planeta) and the Saptamdtrkas (the Seven Divine Mothers), whose origins are as
ancient as they are obecure. The Navagrahaa were believed to direct the destinies
of men, and in order not to offend any of the group by the special worship of one of
the grahas, they were appealed to asa group; but before addressing their eupplica-
tions to the Navagrahas the worshippers considered it propitious to implore the
“Remover of Obstacles’, Vighnantaka, to intervene in their favour. It is probably
for this reason that Ganesa figured in the group; aud that, as he was to be addressed
first before the others, he was represented standing at the extreme right of the
Planets, where he was figured of equal size with them. In all the Navagraha slabs
yet discovered they are imaged standing in a row.®

At this same period Ganeéa, when in attendance on Siva and Parvati, capecially

group of Saptamatrkéa, where he is always represented of equal aize with the god-
deases, seems to indicate that his role waa the same as with the Navagrahas, for the
Seven Divine Mothera were also adored as a group.

According to the Suprabheddgama, Ganeéa should be standing when in the com-
pany of the Saptamétrkas; but in reality he is always seated, as are also the demon
goddesses, and is placed at their extreme left. Aaa rule, his right knee is lifted, and
the left is either pendent or bent before him on the dsana. His head may be crowned
with the kavanda-mukuta as at Ellora, or he may wear the head-dress of Siva, the
jota-mukuta, us at Belur. He is four-armed, holding almost always the axe and
bowl; but the other symbols are not often easily identified. Underneath the dsanas
of the goddesses are niches in which are the vdhanas of the gods of whom they are
the éaktis, while in the niche underneath the seat of Ganeda may be a rat or a bowl
of cakes.

The cult of the Saptamatrkas! is one of the most ancient in India. The seven
Devia were believed to be the feminine counterparts of the Seven Great Brahman
Gods, and their origin is explained in puranic myth as follows: Siva, in a great
battle with the chief of the asuras, Andhasura, found that from every drop of blood
falling to earth from the alain asuras sprang another asura; and fearing to be over-
come by them, he created out of the flame that issued from his mouth in battle a
Sakti, Yogedvari by name. The other seven Brahman Gods did likewise, and the
peven guktis thus brought into existence, led by Yogedvarl, caught each drop of
blood as it fell and thus was the chief of the asuras overcome,

The goddeases were given the names corresponding with the epithets of the Seven
Great Brahman Gods of whom they were the counterparts; and they not oaly
carried their symbols, but, as seen above, were given the same mounts. They were
looked upon as the personitications of the seven bad mental qualities: BrahmAni,
pride; Maheévari, anger; Kaum&ri, illusion; Vaignavi, covetousness; Varahi, envy;
Indrani, fault-finding ; Camunda, tale-bearing; and the leader of the Saptamatrkas,
Yogedvarl, Adma or desire.

Ganeda was placed next to the last goddess at the left, Camunda, who was believed
to partake of the nature of Parvati. In certain villages, especially in south India,
she was to be found in the centre instead of at the left end of the group, and by this
change it was indicated that aupplications were to be addressed to the Saptamatrkds
for increasing the population of the village.

In the Hariraméa there are prayers invoking the Saptamatrkéa who are implored
to protect little children as if they were ‘their real mothers’.? It is perhaps for this
reason that the seven daktis were called the ‘Divine Mothers’, and that in moat of

images of deities; and waa to be meditated upon according to the following dhydna,
as ‘being vermilion of colour, as having three eyes, a large belly, as holding in his
lotus-hands the conch-shell, noose, elephant goad, and as making the sign of bless-
ing. On his forehead shines the young moon.’ He has the head of the King of
Elephants. ...’

After the worshipper has thus invoked Ganeda, he is to offer the pancatativa or the
five elemente,* wine, meat, fish, parched food, and woman, to the Elephant-faced
god who is surrounded by eleven manifestations of himeelf.5 The Divino Mothers
are then to be worshipped, and it is interesting to note here that in the list, in place
of the goddess Indrant, is the goddess Aparajita, whom we shall mect again in
connexion with Ganedéa but under quite different circumstancea.® Before the real
ceremony begins, as we have said above, Ganeéa is invited to depart, by a mantra
of dismissal, after which the Divine Mothers are again worshipped as well as the
Navagrahas and other deities.

The Astasiddhis or the Fight Goddesses believed to preside over Success and
Achievement were looked upon as the Satis of Ganega.’ They are, probably, the
same aa the Saptamatrkda with Yogeévart added; but the author knows of no
Astamatrka slab in India? As will be seen below,® Gancéa had five aspects when
holding his 4aki who may possibly aymbolize this group of Fight Goddesses.

The Saptamatrkas accompanied by Ganeéa were worshipped in Siva shrines ; but
in south India temples were built for the exclusive worship of the group. Those
dedicated to Siva were placed facing the east with Nandi, the bull, at the entrance,
facing west. Images of Ganeéa were always put on the south side of the outer wall
of the linge shrines, and the few temples dedicated to him were built facing south
while the rat was imaged at the entrance looking to the north. Statues of Ganeéa,
however, were more often found in the open,” either at approaches to Siva temples,

In the Mudgala-purdna he is given thirty-two different names, while in tho
Saraddtilaka there are fifty-one dhdranis where he is invoked under different aspects ;
but of these many forms there were alwaya two outstanding features: the face of an
elephant and a fat, thick-set, yaksa-shaped body.

The question ia still unsolved as to how this type sprang into existence, affirming
itaelf without sensible change from the fifth century to the present day. According
to Krishna Deb, he owes the lower part of his body to the yakga cult ; but, as we have
seen above, he was apparently never worshipped as a yakga, for none of his names
are to be found on yaksa lista.

In hie ancient form of Ganapati-Vindyaka we do not know whether he waa
possessed of an elephant-head or not. If, instead of the ganas, Ganeda had been
appointed leader of the pdrisadas, a group of animal-headed inferior deities in the
auite of Siva, his creation with the face of an elephant, ‘ King of Beasta’, would have
found its explanation. There waa also a group of inferior deitics in the suite of Siva
who had another characteristic of Ganeda, being ‘pot-bellied’; but they were in
attendance on Skanda, his brother, according to puranic myths. It seeme, there-
fore, more likely that Ganeda is to be looked upon as 4 derivative rather than as an
original deity. In that case, waa he derived from the Bhairava form of Siva? In the
Visnudharmottara,! Siva, in hia aspect of Bhairava, is described as having a ‘flabby
belly’ (other texta give ‘pot-belly’) and round, yellow (or red) eyes, side-tusks, and
wide nostrils.?, He has for garment the skin of an elephant. In the Vatuka-Bhaira-
wikalpa he is described as red in colour and as having a serpent ‘tied around bis
waist’. Most of these details are characteristics of Ganeda, and the elephant skin
with the head and ita trunk hung over the shoulder of Siva aa Bhairava might easily
have suggested the definite form of Ganega. As a matter of fact, some of his images,
especially in bronze, if studied closely, give the impression of a masked head, which
is atill more apparent in paintings where his face is red and his neck and ears are
pink.® It is of interest to note here that one of his names is ‘Dvi-dehaka’ or *two-
bodied’.

When hia imagea or the seayambhi-mirtis' are daubed with paint, the popular
colour is red; but in paintings he may be either red or yellow, according to the sect,
aad is to be worshipped with offerings of red or yellow flowers. He may also have
the face red and the body yellow; and there are esoteric forms such as Lakemi-
Ganapati and Bhuvaneéa-Ganapati, where he is white. In paintings, he sometimes
carries a red banner on which ia figured his mount, the rat, if not represented beside
him or under his feet, in which case he was called Akhu-ratha, or ‘He who rides on
a rat’. He was seldom imaged, however, seated on the rat;> but in his form of
Heramba, he may be seated on a lion.®

In Nepal the four-headed Ganesa was not unknown, for in a descriptive list of the

five-headed form of Ganeéa was most popular in Nepal and had certain followers
in India; but Heramba is practically never found outside of these two countries.
Certain accounts state that Ganega was born with five heads,‘ one pointing to each
of the four cardinal points with the fifth, on top; and this arrangement of the five
heade was usually adopted for the Heramba form of Ganeéa. There are examples,
however, where, as on a stela found in the Munshiganj district, Dacca, the five heads
are on the same tier, that is, with two on either side of the central head which is
much larger than the other four; but this is a most unusual arrangement.®

When invoked, Ganega was to be conceived as posseasing only one tusk which
should be on the right side. There are, however, images of the Ekadanta with the
tusk on the left side® as well as possessing both tusks.’ He may even be represented
having three tusks, which, however, is most unusual.®

The absence of one of the tusks is variously accounted for in puranic myths; but
the most popular legend gives that it was broken off in an encounter with Siva and
runs as follows: When Parvati created Ganeéa without participation of Siva,’ he
waa thus unknown to the Lord of the Trident who found him guarding the door of
the private apartments of the goddess. Siva challenged him and in the terrific fight
that ensued, one of Ganega’a tusks was broken off by Siva with his axe. There is a
legend, however, which has few followers, claiming that the head of the elephant,
cut off by Visnu and placed on the headless body of Ganeda, had primarily only one
tusk. Certain accounts relate that Ganega used his broken tusk as stylus when acting
as amanuensis to the poet Vyasa.

Avouth Indian legend’ gives another version of the absence of one of the tusks and
accounts for the presence of the rat which usually accompanics Ganeda: there waa
once a giant-demon with the face of an elephant who waa unconqucrable either by
god or by man. Ganeéa, being only a demi-god, was thus able to fight with him with
some possibility of success. At the first encounter the giant-demon, Gajamukhasura
by name, broke off Ganega’s right tusk ; but Ganeda caught his broken tusk and hurled
it at the giant-demon, who instantly turned into a rat, whereupon Ganeéa took him
into his service as his vdhana. One of his ancient names is Mugka-vahana or ‘He
whose mount is a rat’. According to Gupte! the sanskrit word mugka also stands for

As a rule, the trunk was represented hanging straight and only turning to the
left or to the right toward the end; but there are examples where the trunk turns,
almost at once, to the left and then hangs straight? or is raised to the level of the
shoulder? In south India, when it turns to the left, Ganeda is called Idamburi. In
the few examples where it turns to the right, he is called in Tamil Valamburi. When
turned to the left, the trunk, aa a rule, is about to lift cakes (batasa) from a bowl in
Ganega’s left hand, but practically never when turning to the right. The end of the
trunk may be coiled and seemingly balancing a ball-shaped cake called a modaka,*
or is it a jambu (wood-apple) ?

Asa rule, the images of Ganeéa have two eyes, but on Tantric statues and when
invoked in Tantric dhydnas he has a third eye. ‘The two normal eyes, in India, are
represented small, round, staring, and low in relief, while the third eye, set obliquely
in the forehead, is, on the contrary, long and narrow, flame-shaped, and high in
relief. On the forehead may be the tlak of Siva, the crescent moon,* in which case
he ia called Bhilacandra, a form especially worshipped by the esoteric aects. Accord-
ing to legend, the moon, Candra, was cursed by a god and lost ita brightness, where-
upon Ganega picked it up and placed it on his forehead. In place of the moon-tilak,
there may be three horizontal lines* painted white on hia forehead indicating an image

worshipped by a Saiva sect ; but be is popularly adored without tilak or the third eye.

The head, aa a rule, resembles closely that of an elephant; but in Kashmir, and
especially in Nepal, it is represented long, narrow, and flat, while farther east,
especially in Java, the face may take on a more human aspect with the eyes close
together and the trunk seemingly a prolongation of the nose.” ‘The ears are large
and broad as a rule, and each may be ornamented with a conch-shell, as at Ballaur
in the ancient temple of Harihara® as well ag at Deopara, in the Rajshahi District.

The most remarkable of these srayambhi-mirtis in Kashmir is the one on a cliff
along the Kishen-ganiga known as Ganes-Ghati. Sir Aurel Stein writes:? ‘On a face
of grey lime rock, about fifty feet high, nature has formed a long projecting nose
which curiously resembles the head of an elephant with the trunk hanging down.
The rock is covered in this place with the orthodox daub of red paint. This shows
plainly where pious tradition places the head of the elephant-faced god which has
given its name to the hill.’ .

In the mythical land of Jambu-dvipa there was a mountain in the shape of an
elephant called Vinataka, often confused with Vindyaka, The reault was that in
every Buddhist country where there was a hill or mountain which was vaguely in
the form of the head of an elephant, the worship of Ganeda was set up and a place
of pilgrimage established. ‘Thus, from an unimportant déity, Ganega, ‘Remover of
Obstacles’, grew in popularity and waa taken up not only by the Buddhist but by
all the Hindu yects. Up to the tenth century, he waa rarely worshipped alone and
waa combined with practically every important deity in the Hindu pantheon; but
although he occupied a prominent place in Brahman mythology and was worshipped
as one of the five Great Brahman Gods, there ia no mention, even in late purdnas,
of his being invested with special divine power. Under the name of Vinayak,
Ganeéa was adored, however, by all those who embarked upon any enterprise; and
as Vighnesgvara, he was invoked at the beginning of every book to ensure literary
success. His image was placed on the site of a future construction and a puja
performed with offcrings of flowers, to gain his benign guardianship. As popular
legend gave him the reputation of robbing pioua worsbippera of the fruit of their
devotions if not properly propitiated, he was always invoked before beginning the

devotions. There was, however, one mantra according to the Mahdnirvdna-tantra,?
the sacred Hrahmd-gayatri, which might be recited without first appealing to
“Ganega the Thief”.

In south India, expecially, he is a popular household god and is familiarly adored
as Pillaiyar.* He ia invoked the first of all the gods at the morning ablutione and
again at noon and before sleeping at night; aud is often worshipped in company
with four other great deities: Vignu, Siva, Parvati, and Aditya, the Sun, symbol of
Brahma. In high-caste houses, there is usually a sacred receptacle containing a
small altar for the five household gods.’ If Siva was chosen as the chief household
god, he waa placed in the centre, in his liga form, with Nandi the bull facing him.
Ganesa was seated cither on the other eide of the liviga or in one of the comers, while

Across his body, from under the right arm to across the left shoulder, was either

the Brahmanical thread or a serpent in its place; and around the belly was often
tied a second girdle of a anake,! the presence of which is accounted for in the follow-
ing puranic legend: Ganeda, receiving from his worshippers an offering of innumer-
able ball-shaped cakes (modaka), proceeded to eat them all and thus his helly became
abnormally distended. He then mounted on his wihana, the rat,? and started off to
return to his dwelling place, when the rat, seeing a serpent cross its path, tripped
with fright, sending (tanega rolling to the ground. In falling off, his dilated abdomen
burst, and all the innumerable modakas that he had eaten fell out. He proceeded to
gather them and replace them in his capacious belly ; and after killing the serpent,
he tied it around his waist to keep them from falling out again. The Moon, sur-
rounded by his twenty-seven starry consorts, had watched the above proceeding
with much mirth and finally burst into uprosrious laughter, which so infuriated
Ganeda that he pulled out one of his tusks and threw it at the Moon. Darknegs then

covered the earth, and the gods in great distress begged the ‘Remover of Obstacles’

to withdraw his tusk from the repentant Moon. This he consented to do, but in

order to punish the Moon, he willed that for ever after ita brilliance, in every month,
should wax and wane.

With the growing influence of the Tantras and the popularity of Saktism, Ganeda
was imaged, as were the other gods, in company with hia female counterpart whom
be was represented holding with his left arm, either beside him or on his left hip.
The devi, as a rule, had her right arm around his neck, while the left, as we have aaid
above, often held the bow! of batasas.

Saktiem was particularly practised by a powerful sect who adopted Ganeda as
their special deity and established his worship to the exclusion of practically every
other Hindu god, even of his parente, Siva and Parvati. Ganeda under the name of
Ganapati thus became an important deity toward the teuth century when the
Génapatya sect set up the cult of five Sakti-Ganapatia called: Ucchigta-Ganapati,
four-armed, red in colour; Mahd-Ganapati, ten-armed, red; Urddhva-Ganapati,
aix-armed, yellow; Piigala-Ganapati, six-armed ;3 Lakam!-Gana pati, four- or eight-
armed, white, while the éakti is yellow and carries a lotus.*

In time, the Ganapatya divided into six separate sects, and while they were all
six of accord in looking upon Ganeéa as the Firat Great Cause ‘through whose mdyd
Siva and the other gods were created’, he waa nevertheless worshipped by each sect
under a different name, in a somewhat different form, and differently worded
mariras were used for their respective mystic rites of initiation. They set up two
ways of worshipping Ganapati which varied only in detail, according to the sect:

1. By mystical contemplation.

Those of the Ganapatya who worshipped Ganeéa under the aspect of Maha-

Ganapati were to meditate upon his image, red of colour with ten arms, holding

The eect which adored the Elephant-faced One under the form of Haridra-

devotee was branded on both arma with hia elephant face having only one tusk.

Another meditative sect of the Ganapatya adored him aa Ucchista-Ganapati, but
unlike the above exclusive sects, all castes were admitted to his worship. The
devotecs meditated on his form with four arms, holding a pomegranate in one of his
hands while embracing his sakti. He was to be conceived while practising most
licentious rites and ceremonies.

2. By devotional practices without meditation.

The devotee, after crowning Ganeda with flowers, was to make offerings of fruit
and of red or yellow flowers, according to the sect.

The Ganapatya were linga worshippers and the Ganapati liriga? was considered
second only to the Svayambhn, which was enshrined in the innermoat sanctuary of
8 temple and never gazed upon by its worshippers. The Ganapati liriga, being leas
sacred, was shown to the members of the Ganapatya sect. It was in form somewhat
in the shape of a citron,’ a cucumber, or a wood apple‘ and its worship was believed
to have been set up by the ganas at a very early date

At the annual féte of the Ganapatya, Ganeéa waa sometimes represented with his
father, Siva, in attendance on him ; but outside of the six esoteric secta, the exclusive
worship of Ganeda was not practised.

In the Samaydcdra-tantra® are mentioned the six dmndyas or faces of Siva: four
facing the cardinal points, one on top and the sixth, underneath and hidden, and
from whose mouth were spoken the sddhanas and siddhimantras. From the face
looking toward the west, called Kama or love, were revealed the goda Krigna,
Ganeda, Yama and Sorya, Candra and other planets. The Tantra-éastra refers to
the six minds of the six heads of Siva; but in the Vardha-purdna there ia only ques-
tion of one face, from the forehead of which issued, as we have said above, a great
brilliance in which Ganoéa was ‘revealed’, that is, ‘mind-born’.

Thus certain esoteric sects came to look upon Ganeda as a material manifestation
of the manas of Siva, manifestation which, as seen above in the Ganapatya Hymn
was conceived to be made up of earth, water, fire, air, and ether. In India, the five
elements were symbolized by the elemental stipa which was composed of a rect-
angle (earth), circle (water), triangle (fire), crescent (air), anda flame-shaped symbol
rising from the centre of the crescent (ether).’ It ia a curious fact that the seated
esoteric form of Ganeda vaguely suggests an clemental stipa if we conceive of the
rectangle as being formed hy the thick, elephant-shaped legs bent beneath the god;

complicated Tantric representationa may have ten or more. The symbols that he may
carry and which are held by no other deity are: the broken tusk, a citron, wood-
apple (jambu), a radish, a stylus, a bow! of cakes, and a modaka; but he may have
other attributes such as the axe, a lasso, a book, a sword, a kalada, a snake, &c.)
In his Tantric forms with many arms, he holds the usual Tantric symbols, The
broken tusk and the axe have been accounted for above, in puranic myths, The
citron or the jambu stands for the Ganapati-liiga. The radish, almoat unknown in
Jodie, is a favourite attribute in Nepal and in Tibet as well as in Japan. As it ia
never carried by Ganega at the same time as the broken tusk, which is practically
unknown in those three countries, it seems possible that the radish waa primarily
a misinterpretation of a badly executed broken tusk. There is a saying in Bengal
that Ganega is the god with ‘cars like a fan and teeth like a radish’ ;? but outside
of Japan, the author knows of no legend explaining ite presence in his hand.

He may also carry the water-vessel (kalasa) either in one of his hands or it
may be held in his trunk, in which latter case it is more likely a wine jar, for in a
dhyana in the Mahdnirwina-tantra it is said: ‘His great trunk is adorned with the
jar of wine which it holds’. The stylus and the book, ax we have seen above, may be
found in paintings in India, while in Indo-China there are several ancient statuca
depicting him as a scribe. The bowl of cakes may be said to be not only his most
ancient but his most characteristic attribute. It ia almost always held in his left
hand, and the trunk is often represented lifting one of the small cakes called batasa
from the bowl. If figured with his éadti, ahe may hold the bowl. The modaka is aleo
a cake, ball-shaped but larger than the batasa, and iz usually represented by itself.
It may cither be held in the coil of the trunk or be placed before him on the dsana ;*
but with the exception of Bala-Ganapati’ and Taruna-Ganapati, none of his forms
carry it in the hands, It is sometimes held in the mouth of the rat.® Certain secta
looked upon the modaka as aymbolizing Maha-Buddhi or Supreme Wisdom; but in
the esoteric doctrine practised by the Ganapatyas, it was conceived ag representing
the germ of life.” Rao makes reference to this mystic interpretation when he explains
that in the esoteric doctrine the round belly of Ganega, Lord of the Universe, was
imbued with mystic significance, that is, that it was to be conceived ag symbolizing
4 vast expanse of space, capacious enough to hold the thousands of modakaa which

stood for all the human germs in the universe.'®

paintings of Ganesa,’ the modaka is replaced by the cintémani, which in Mahayana
Buddhism is the magic jewel interpreted, in the esoteric doctrine, as symbolizing
the germ of life. In India, however, Ganesa practically never holds the magic jewel ;
but in the India Museum in London there is a small bronze image where, in his right
hand, he balances a modaka distinctly in the form of a cintamant, while his left arm is
around the waist of the dakti, Ganeda, nevertheless, is addressed in the Arya-Ganapati-
stotra? as ‘Lord of Jewels, with a body of Jewels, Jewelled Source of Jewels, who
protects the Jewels of the Doctrine. O, Lord, with the trunk of an elephant, ho-
mage’; and in the Ganesa-purdna there is a legend which describes Ganesa as being
symbolized by a cintamani A great Asura, Tripurasura by name, worshipped
Gancda with the two mantras, ‘Sri Ganesa namah* and ‘Ont’, while standing on his
great toe thousands of years in mystic contemplation of the Elephant-faced god.
Ganesa, deeply impressed by such austerity, appeared to him, whereupon Tripur-
asura asked aa reward for his victory over his senses and passions that he be able to
conquer the Three Worlds. As soon as Ganeéa had granted hia request, he,proceeded
to conquer Brahma and to reduce Vignn to submission; but Siva, when asked to
descend from Mount Kailasa, demurred. The three great gods then appealed to
Ganesa to save them from utter destruction. Ganeda, promising to do so, disguised
himself as a Brahman ascetic and, appearing to Tripurdsura, proposed building for
him a great city on Mount Kailasa, but insisted that, at the entrance to Kailasa, be
erected a cintdmani dedicated to Ganeéa. Tbia being done, Siva refused to abandon
Kailisa and a great battle ensued; but aa the Lord of the Trident put his faith in
Ganega and worshipped him, Tripurdsura lost the Three Worlds and disappeared
into the Divine Essence, as had been promised him by the Supreme Ganeéa.

Ganeéa was usually represented seated, but in his most ancient images he was
more often figured standing. If sitting, he may have the pose called mahérdajalila
or ‘royal ease’, that is, with one of the knees lifted while the other ia bent before
him on the dsana; or he may be seated with crossed legs or with one leg pendant.
In Java, both of the legs are bent before him with the soles of the feet touching;
but the feet are neither human nor do they resemble absolutely those of an elephant.*
If he is standing, he is either straight (sama-bhanga) or may have two bends (dvi-
bhanga), three bends (tri-bhatiga), or be in dancing attitude.® He was represented
either without ornaments or as wearing many jewels, which in certain forms were
replaced by snakes or, as in Java, by skulls, Except when figured as a child, he was
usually imaged ‘pot-bellied’ (Lambodara) and bare to the waist, with a girdle
below the abdomen holding the garment that covered his hips and that varied
according to the country in which the image was conceived. In Nepal, it was often
a stiff-pleated skirt hanging to below the knees; in Chinese Turkestan, tight-fitting
trousers called paijdmas; at Tun-huang, full, many-folded trousers; in Indo-China,
the sampot ; but in India, he was either nude or wearing a dhoti, with sometimes a
tiger skin covering his hips.

If Ganesa was chosen as the principal household god, he was placed in the centre
of the altar with Visnu in the right-hand corner of the prescribed square, but at the
back, while Siva was in the corresponding left-hand corner. At the right-hand
corner, in front, was Parvati, while in the corresponding left-hand comer was
Aditya.

When invoking Ganega, the worshipper was to conceive of an island made of nine
precious stones, ... In that island are wish-fulfilling trees and creepers like manddra
and parijata... the horizon is illuminated by the rising sun and the moon. ‘Conceive
a parijata tree (one of the five trees of paradise) made of nine jewels... . Under the
tree, inside the primordial lotus, is an dsana seated upon which Maba-Ganapati
should be invoked inside a triangle, within a hexagon. . . .’?

CHAPTER IIL

INDIA: GANESA IN HINDU SCULPTURES AND PAINTINGS

Ga is not to be found in sculpture before the Gupta period, when his

image appeared not only suddenly but in the classic form by which he may be
identified froin the fifth century up to the present day. It seems incredible that the
remarkable Bhumara sculptures of Ganeéa! should have been created independently,
without the inspiration of transitional forms; and yet no images of an ciephant-faced
deity have been discovered which could be placed unquestionably earlier than the
fifth century. ‘The explanation may possibly be found if we apply the same theory
to the ancient images of Ganeéa, as to certain archacological ruins in India that are
believed to have been reproduced from more ancient monuments which were con-
structed in impermanent material. In that case, the question would arise as to
whether the original form of the elephant-faced deity was conceived in north or in
south India.

Coomaraswamy as well aa Jouveau-Dubreuil are inclined to look upon an image
on one of the Amaravati railings,? aaid to be not later than the beginning of our era,
az a tranaitional form of Ganeda. The personage is crouching under the weight of
a long, serpent-shaped garland,’ upheld at intervals by other ganas. Only a part
of the body is left, but enough remains to show that the gana is short and of the
usual yaksa corpulence. The head is unquestionably that of an elephant: that is,
the eyes, ears, and the lower lip; but as the image has neither trunk nor tusk, it is
questionable whether it is really a prototype of Ganega. On the other hand, in Ceylon,
near Mihintale, a ettipa has been recently excavated on which there is a frieze of ganas
in the style of those of Amarivatt, and one of the ganas has the face of an elephant,
complete with trunk and tusk.* Paranavitane describes them as seemingly supporting
the superstructures of the vahalkadas or ‘frontispieces’ which usually decorate the
Sinhalese stipas. The elephant-faced gana is seated with attendant ganas on either
side facing him ; and from the fact that he has only one tusk, Paranavitane is of the
opinion that the image may be a prototype of the classical Ganeda. In that case it
would be the most ancient representation of Ganeda vet found, for this stdpa, called
Kantaka Cetinga, ia referred to in inscriptions of the first and second centuries.
Even if, as he believes, the sculptures are of a eomewhat later period, the image may
be unquestionably ascribed to the firat centuries of our era; but as it is the only one
of its kind and is in a group of other ganas, it could hardly have struck the popular
imagination deeply enough to inspire the forins of Ganega which appeared in India
in the Gupta period. Thus, seemingly without transitional stage, the most ancient
representations of Ganeda are the completed type: two or four-armed, holding the
axe, the broken tusk, a modaka (or is it a jambu?), or a bowl of cakes. ;

form of Ganeéa known to the author in India.

As we have seen above, Ganeda is not only to be found at the extreme left of the
group of the Saptamatrkas, but was alao sometimes imaged in company with the
Navagrahas (the Nine Planets), where he was always placed at the extreme right,
next to the sun, Ravi. A carved Navagraha slab was found near the ancient mins
of Kankandighi,’ where the images of the Nine Planets are represented standing in
a row with Ganesa, at the right, wearing the high jata-mukuta of Siva and carrying
a roeary and battle-axe. These slabs have also been found in other districts, auch
as in the Orissan temples, where the Navagraha slabs were placed on the doorways
as architraves; while in south India they were found in the enclosed verandahs
around the main shrine. Only two Navagraha slabs have been found in Bengal with
the image of Ganeéa accompanying the Nine Planets; but it is not known where
these slabs were originally placed since there is no ancient temple in Bengal atill
standing. They were assuredly put in the temples as a protection from evil and
possibly to ensure prosperity to the temple’s founders.

At Ghatiydla, near Jodhpur, there is an ancient column which is of special
interest in that its inscription, dated a.p. 862, is the earliest dated inscription in
India in praise of the Elephant-faced god. On the top of the column are four images
of Ganeda seated back to back, facing the four cardinal points and possibly repre-
senting the dig-gajas or the four elephants supposed to support the heavens in the
four directions of the compass.

An unusual image of Ganeda waa found in the Tripura state in Bengal. The
colossal sculptures, which are said to date between the eleventh and twelfth cen-
turies, were carved in the perpendicular rock of the Unakoti hill, where a celebrated
Saiva sanctuary drew followers of Siva in the eighth and ninth centuries. At the
extreme left of the group is a seated, four-armed figure of Ganesa 30 feet high,?
while beside him are two standing elephant-headed attendants, having four tusks
and four and six arms. They carry none of the attributes of Ganeéa, but hold a
wheel, drum, hell, &c., and have conch-shells in their cars? and while Ganega, nude
to the waist, with a serpent belt holding in place a dhoti draped over his hips, is
represented with the usual corpulence, the two standing figures have most attenu-

Ganesa was sometimes figured in attendance on other important deities besides
Siva, as for example on an ancient sculptured slab discovered at Sonarang, where
the Sun-god, Sarya,! is represented accompanied by Ganeda on the right and the
Saptamatrkas on the left. Above the Sun-god are the Navagrahas, who also figure
abovo Siva in the sculpture representing the Vaivéhika-marti.?

At Trichinopoly, as well as at Vallam, there are bas-reliefa of Ganeéa believed to
be of the seventh century. At Trichinopoly, placed near the Siva shrine, he is
figured standing; while at Vallam, he is seated wearing a karanda-mukuta and
balancing a modaka at the end of his trunk that is turned to the right, which is
most unusual.* None of the attributes in the four hands may he identified with the
exception of the lower right, which is possibly the broken tusk. He is seated in the
mahdrdjalila pose, that is, with the right knee lifted and the left leg bent and repos-
ing on the throne parallel with his body. It is an attitude seldom used for Ganeéa,
probably on account of the difficulty that it presented to the sculptor because of his
obesity. At any rate, this seventh-century bas-relief is probably the earliest repre-
sentation of Ganesa in the mahdrdjalild attitude.

Over the doorways of the Dakhan temples dedicated to Siva and above the
dedicatory block, was always placed an image of Ganeda, just as, in the Chalukyan
temples, Gaja-Laksmt* was the presiding goddess. In the fine temple of Balsane
in Khandesh, the east shrine contained the liaga, while over the doorwaye of the
south and north shrines were images,of Ganesa. At Patna, above the dedicatory
block and again above the image of Ganesa, is the groap of Saptamatrkas accom-
panied by Ganeéa, always at the left, and Siva at the right.

A sculpture of great intereat was discovered in Bengal, at Dhanuka, which is of
importance because it seems to indicate a transition stage from the classic Hindu
form of Ganeéa to that showing Mahayana Buddhist influence.* He is seated on a
lotus rising from the water which is vaguely indicated, while at the left is a lotus-
bud and at the right a small lotus-flower on which reposes hia right foot. His left
leg ia bent before him on the padmdsana, which is represented as in Mahayana
Buddhist images with the last row of lotus-petals tumed downward. The Ggure of
Ganeéa, however, is Hindu in style. He wears the high jaté-mukuta of Siva and many
jewels: necklace, bracelets, anklets, aa well as the Brahmanical thread. He has four
arms. In his upper right he holda a symbol which is unknown, as a rule, in India:
a large radish, attribute which is frequently met with on the Buddhist images of
Ganeda in Nepal and Tibet. As the pointed, broken tusk is sekiom found in the
hands of a Nepalese or Tibetan figure of Ganeda, and as he is never Tepresented in
