At Badami, the ancient capital of the Chalukyan kings, there are three series of
Brahmanical rock-cut temples dating from the time of Pulikesi I. In the first of
these is the celebrated sculpture of Siva and Parvati as ardhandrisa, that is, figured
aa one deity, the right half Siva and the left side Parvatt.' Burgess explains that it
is ‘a single body personifying the principle of life and production in ita double aspect:
the male or active principle under the name of purusha and the female or passive,
under the name of prakrti’. It will be scen below that Ganeda was given this double
aspect in China and Japan but differently presented.’

Outside of the Saiva grotto-temple at Badami and to the left of the pillared
gallery is a spirited bas-relief of Siva dancing the ta@rdava, while at hia feet is a small
image of Ganeda, the only representation at Badami.* He is standing in a alightly
dancing pose. Behind his head is a halo, and he has four arms of which two are
broken. ‘Che normal left hand holds an attribute which may be a bowl of cakes,
while the upper right seems to follow the movement of the dance.

In the third of these cave-temples is an inscription dated a.p. 579. Fergusson is
of the opinion that if not contemporary, the other two were certainly executed
before a.p. 700; but although this image of Ganeda is thus the most ancient of all
those found in grotto-temples in India, it is later than the sculpture of Ganesa in
the Chinese rock-cut temple of Kung-hsien, which is dated a.v. 531.4

At Aihole, where the rock-cut temples are believed to be among the most ancient
in India, aa well as at Badami, Ganesa is often found in attendance on Siva when
dancing the taedava.* In thia case also he is figured standing in a slightly dancing
pose. At Aihole he is lifting a cake with his trunk from a bow! in his left hand.
This is possibly the earliest example of the attitude which later was adopted in
practically every country where he waa worshipped.

Jn the Dakhan the group of the Saptamatrks was in favour as early as the fifth
century; and the popularity of Ganeda associated with them is evident in all the
temples and rock-cut sanctuaries.

In the cave-temples of Ellora, which are believed to be slightly later than those
at Badamt, there are four important sculptures of the group of Ganesa and the
Seven Divine Mothers which were executed between the fifth and the tenth cen-
turies, The most remarkable of the groups is in the celebrated Ravana-ka khai,* where
each Divine Mother, four-armed, holds a child (with the exception of Maheévart) ;
and ina niche under each throne is the mount of the deity, but Ganega has a bowl
of cakes in the niche instead of the usual rat.

The image of Ganeda in the Ravana-ka khai’ is as powerful in conception as the
two Bhumara sculptures. The ears, trunk, and legs resemble those of the Ganesa
with the bells so closely that it seems possible, if they were of about the same period,
that they were executed by the same person. They may be compared as to dignity

In the Lankeavara of the Kailasa grotto-temple there is a curious statue which
seems to be unique of its kind. The goddess Uma (Parvati) stands between two

The sane arrangement of deities was in favour in the Gujarat; and the Elephant-
faced god was held in such favour that he was even to be found in Jain grotto-

with one leg pendant.

In central India at Bhera-ghat near Jubbulpur, there is a circular temple dedi-
cated to the sixty-four Yoginfs (now called Gauri-Satikara) which is ascribed to

of the Haihayas of Tripurt.

Tnside the temple is a most interesting bas-relief of Ganega,'? who is imaged four-

On hia head is an ornate mukufa, and above his forehead that is high and flat like

seen on images of Ganeda in Nepal but little known in India."! Kis trunk turns to
the left in an unusual curve and then tums to the right, holding a modaka in the
curled end.

The temple of the Gaurt-Sankara is surrounded by a circular arcade in which are
seated the sixty-four yoginis, unfortunately much mutilated. Among these images
is a Ganesani, a female form of Ganega."? Unlike Ganega, she has an attenuated

attributes, at least, never those indicated above.

Probably one of the earliest images of Ganesa is a small terra-cotta bas-relief
which was found at the ancient site of Akra, N.W.F.P., where pre-Gupta objects aa
early as the second century have been discovered.” The figure of Ganesa, however,
is certainly not earlier than the fifth century, and those found at Parkarhar are
possibly still Ister. In both casea, the sinall burnt-clay images, which were probably
ex-votos, represent Ganeéa in a slightly dancing attitude, holding a round object
that may be a modaka.

An image of Ganesa which was found in the Fatehgarh district and ia believed to
have come originally from Sankisa Mound may be the most ancient representation
of the god in tone as yet discovered.? The slab, on which is carved the figure of
Ganega in high relief, is of the spotted atone peculiar to Mathura, and is not more
than twenty inches in height. Tt is summarily carved and strangely proportioned.
The bare head with huge ears ia abnormally large, while the nude torso ia too short
for the length of the arms. The legs seem to end at the knees, giving the impression
of a seated figure which, however, is not the case. The right arm is bent and grasps
8 pointed object which is probably the tusk. The left, also bent, holds the bow! of
cakes, The trunk, unlike the Indian representations, where it hangs straight and
only coils to the left to reach the bowl, turns almost at once to the left, and then
hangs straight to the bowl. The author knows of only one other example of this
unusual curve of the trunk in the image discovered at ‘Taol Phak Kin in Indo-China.*

A curious statue, which seems to be unique, was found at Bhumara and is attri-
buted to the fifth century.5 In spite of the fact that the symbols are missing, it is
undoubtedly an image of Ganesa, for although the trunk ia broken off at the end, it
turns to the left and can easily be conceived as lifting a cake from a bowl that should
be in the left hand, which ia also missing. The unusual feature of the figure is a
chain of round-shaped bells* which hangs across the breast, and there are bell
ornaments on the karanda head-dresa, bracelets, and anklets.

Coomaraswamy is of the opinion that it represents a yaksa-form of Ganeéa ;? and
he refers to an ancient Tibetan legend as possibly explaining the presence of the
bells: A doorkeeper or dudrapala of Vaisall, having died, waa reborn as a demon.
He begged the inhabitants of Vaidall to put up a statue to him in the form of a yakga
and to hang a bell around his neck 30 that whenever they were in danger from their
enemies he might sound the alarm by ringing the bell. This was done with great
pomp and ceremony, and the inhabitants of Vaisall were ever after protected from
their enemies by the foresight of the yaksa divirapala.®

It is not impossible that the above statue of Ganeda was destined to serve as

Tn these two Bhumira sculptures, he seems to have been conceived aa independent

» — In the Chalukyan temples, Ganega was represented at the extreme left of the

Ganega ia the rat.é

In the Chalukyan rock-cut temples, on the contrary, there are no examples of the
Seven Divine Mothers. Ganeéa is figured in attendance on Siva and never indepen-

In the Munshiganj district, Dacca, a stela of the eleventh or twelfth century was

found where Ganega is represented in a form seldom met with outside of Nepal.

He has five heads, but the arrangement of the heads in a circle differs again from the

Nepalese Heramba? in that while the central head is normal in size, the two heads
on either side are much smaller, their trunks, which hang over the shoulders, barely
reaching to the breasts. Another difference is that the fifth head of the Nepalese
Heramba is always on top. Above the image of Heramba is a group of four one-
headed, two-armed Gancéaa, with (slightly lower down, ) another Ganega on either side
which completes the group of seven, counting the Heramba. Like the Nepalese
form, he is seated on a lion and haa ten arms holding the usual symbols; but unlike
the Heramba in Nepal, his forehead ia broad and the eves on the central head,
placed far apart, are set obliquely with a protuberance between them euch as will
be met with in Indo-China.‘ At Negapatam, there is a remarkable bronze statue in

discovered where Ganesa in dancing pose’ ix figured, on the contrary, as in Nepal,
with the head long and narrow, but with this difference—that the bridge of the nose
is well defined and the trunk seems to be the prolongation of the nose. The author
knows of no other example of this aspect of the trunk outside of Borneo,’ nor is she
able to explain the presence of this strange and unique figure of Ganega in India.

Another interesting and ancient sculpture of Ganeéa was found in the Chamba

state near the Balenl pass, representing him under an aspect which is perhaps unique.
A sanctuary was discovered, dedicated to the serpent god Satahr, popularly wor-

Beside the slab of the Naga was found a defaced image of Ganeéa, who was called
by the worshippers ‘gedlu’ or ‘Cowherd’ of the Naga,® and many ex-votos testified
to his popularity. This humble aspect was possibly based on an ancient legend
where Brahma, believing the ten-necked Ravana to be a negligible deity, once
promised him, on account of hia austerities, that he should never be conquered by
ged or super-man, Ravana, now practically invulnerable, set out to subjugate the
gods and the Three Worlds. According to one of the versions, when Ravana sub-
jugated Ganeda, he forced him to work for him as a cowherd. From a simple cowherd,
Ganeéa became * Protector of Cowherds’ when certain sects, aa we have seen above,
set up his worship as a human manifestation of Krisna, ‘Protector of the Gopas and

CHAPTER IV
INDIA, NEPAL, CHINESE TURKESTAN, TIBET: GANESA IN
BUDDHISM
Te. popularity of Gageds as Siddhidata, Beatower of Success, was not confined
to the Brahman cult, for his adoration was taken over by the Buddhists, who
claimed that a mystic mantra in praise of Ganega, called the Ganapati-hrdaya,' was
disclosed to Ananda by the Buddha himeelf at Rajagrha. They personified the
mantra in the form of a goddess named Ganapatibrdaya, who, according to Bhatta-
charyya,? was probably looked upon aa the dakti of Ganeéa. The mantra, however,
refers only to Ganega and contains a addhana to be used in hig invocation, when he
is to be conceived aa being red of hue, standing in a dancing attitude, as having
twelve arms holding Tantric symbols, and as possessed of a third eye as well as of
both his tusks.

In a Nepalese leaf-book of the fifteenth century there is a miniature of Ganeda
as described above, with the sddhana to be used in his invocation.” The priest, after
contemplating this image (having previously prepared himself for the ceremony
by fasting &c.), should recite the Ganapati-Ardaya-manira which begins: Namo
Bhagavate Aryaganapatihrdaydya ; and after invoking (?aneéa, he should put before
him the desiderata of hia client. The mantra was believed to be most powerful, and
when recited by the pricat or worn aa an amulet waa said to be infallible in obtaining
all that was desired.

There ia, however, no mention of Ganeéa in the Nepalese Buddhist leaf-book of
the beginning of the eleventh century which ia in the Cambridge University Library,
nor is he represented in the miniatures of the celcbrated Buddhist leaf-book of about
this period which is in the library of the Asiatic Society of Bengal. We know, never-
theless, that he was taken over by the Buddhists before this time.

His image may be found on Buddhist sculptures of the late Gupta period such as
at Sémnath, where, on a small fragment representing the death of the Buddha, he ia
figured on his rat among the deities assisting at the parinirudna of the Master.‘ Hoe
may not be considered, however, as a Buddhist deity in this instance, since bo is
figured in # group with his brother, Karttikeya, the Navagrahas, end other Brahman
deities ; but it is of special intereat to find him in this Buddhist sculpture because it
furnishes a transition stage between hie Hindu and Buddhist representations.

We find him in later Buddhist art in India, not only in the form of the Buddhist
god Vinayaka, but in a Hindu demon form also called Vindyaka. In the later role
he was not represented in India aa in Nepal and Tibet, under the feet of an important
deity, but as crouching on all four, under the lotus throne of a fighting Buddhist
divinity. Stone images have been found in Bengal where Ganeda, the Hindu demon,
is thus figured under the padmésana of the Buddhist goddess Bhykut!-Tara’ aa well ag

under the lotus throne of Parpaéavari.! In this latter case he is prostrate, holding both
the sword and shield, and is evidently to be conceived as vanquished by the goddess.

As the Buddhist god, Vinayaka, he was represented in a dancing attitude and is
rarely met with except in paintings. This form is called Nytta-Ganapati and was
not only popular in north India but was adopted in Nepal and found its way
into Tibet.

In India, the Nrtta-Ganapati if painted is to be conceived as yellow in colour. Ho
is figured with the right leg bent with the foot on the lotus throne; while the left,
somewhat more bent, holds the foot in the air in a dancing attitude. The Hindu
form haa usually four arms but may have as many as eight, carrying the usual
aymbols—tusk, bowl of cakes, lasso, elephant goad, &c.—but having one arm free
to follow the movement of the dance.

In Nepal, on the contrary, the Nytta-Ganapati is to be conceived as red of hue.?
He docs not stand directly on the lotua throne but on his tdhana, the rat, green in
colour and holding in its mouth a cinéémani. The right leg is represented as in the
Indian form, while the left, on the contrary, is bent almost double, the foot touching
the serpent girdle. He has a third eye and wears a crown omamented with cin-
tamanis. His usual form haa twelve arms and carries Tantric symbols as weil as the
eymbols that are held by the Hindu form, with the two following exceptions. The
Nepalese form seldom carries the tusk but in its place is usually figured a radish,
possibly, as suggested above, through a misinterpretation of the Hindu symbol.
The second exception is the modaka, which, as we have also said above, is replaced
by a cinéémani in Nepal; and instead of wearing a garland of skulls, in this Tantric
form like the dharmapalas he has a garland of cintémanis falling to the knees.

The Nytta-Ganapati is represented in a Nepalese painting surrounded by four
Vinayakas,> making an assembly of five deities, which fact seems to indicate a
Buddhist rather than a Brahman group for two reasons: first, because five was 6
favourite number with the Mahdyanists for grouping their gods; secondly, because
each deity is of a different colour which was invariably the case in a group of five
deities of Mab’yana Buddhism. There is 2 Buddhist temple in Nepal dedicated to
the Five Ganapatis, where possibly the fifth and central figure is Sarya-Vindyaka,
If, however, the miniature referred to above ia Brahmanic, the central figure may be
Ganapati-Vinayaka, surrounded by four manifestations of himeelf.

It is not known at what date the worship of Ganeéa was first introduced into
Nepal, Lut as referred to above, there is a legend to the effect that the daughter of
the Buddhist king Agoka founded a temple in Nepal which waa dedicated to him.‘
Near Zimpi-Taudu there is an ancient Nepalese temple dedicated to Ganeda® in
which there are stelas with inscriptions dating from the eighth to the tenth centuries;
but the actual date of the foundation of the temple is unfortunately unknown. It
is safe to say, nevertheless, that already by the tenth century the popularity of
Ganega was gaining ground in Nepal as in India; and that Vindyaka, ‘Remover of

The Nepalese Buddhists had their own legends with regard to the FElephant-faced
god and claimed that he manifested himself to a mythical king, Vikramajit by
name, to whom he brought untold blessings, mostly in the form of riches.

None of the Hindu legends in regard to the creation of Ganeda was accepted by
the Nepalese. Neither Siva nor Parvati waa believed to have participated in his
advent, for, of his own free will, he became visible in @ ray of sunshine; hence his
name Strya-Vindyeka. The Brahmans claimed that he appeared in this form in
order to bring to life the son of a Brahman.!

Little is known, however, in regard to the representation of Sarya-Vindyaka, who
was always worshipped unaccompanied by other gods like the esoteric images of
Ganeda in India. It is possible that thia form was Tantric, for the ceremonica in
connexion with his worship, that is, offerings of coagulated blood and bloody
sacrifices, would indicate Tantric ritea; but in the principal sanctuary of a temple
dedicated to Strya-Vinayaka there is an image of Ganapati where nothing indicates
a Tantric form, should this be his actual manifestation as Surya-Vinayaka. He ia
represented in the classic fashion, with one head and four arms, holding an are, bowl
of cakes, broken tusk, and rosary, and is standing on the rat.

The Nepalese imagea of Ganapati, with the exception of his Heramba form, are
one-headed, and the elephant face has this particularity in Nepal that it is long and
narrow and rather flat with the eyes aet obliquely. There is often the third eye or
else, especially if the image belongs to the Hindu cult, a Saiva (ilaka of three hori-
zontal lines. In fact, Ganega may have both the third eye and thefilaka. He Poasesscs
many aris, ranging from four to eixteen, and may be represented seated, but is
more often standing, and aaa rule, on the rat. He may wear a curious pleated skirt
with ornate hangings.”

At Katmandu there are two unusual stone statues of Ganeéa with a rat under
each foot.* Both images are one-headed but one has four, whereas the other bas
sixteen, hands, and both embrace the 4akti with the nomnai left arm.

Ganeda wearing the serpent girdle and serpent Brahmanical thread is common to
all countries where he is worshipped ; but Ganesa holding the serpent-god Sega over
his head like Siva in one of his dancing forms as Nataraja,‘ with few exceptions is
known only to Nepal.® In both cases the head of the serpent is grasped in the upper
right hanct and the tail in the upper left.

Doubtlesa the Nepalese were acquainted with the north India dancing form of
Siva holding the ndga as a canopy over his head; and it is therefore not surprising
to find the serpent Sega adopted in Nepal for certain representations of Ganega in
dancing attitude. As Siva’s son, he was sometimes given the jewelled head-dress
of Siva, the kiritasnukula as well aa the crescent-moon-tilaka, while in Tibet and

Herambza, on his lion mount, was perhaps the most popular form of Ganesa in
Nepal, where he was represented with ten arms, and as a rule, embracing his éakti.!
The Heramba form always has five heads, which are either arranged as in India in
two tiers —-one head above the central head which has a head on either side and one
at the back—or an arrangement of heads in three tiers, which form is only found in
Nepal—a head on either side of the central head which has a head above it and still
another above the head on the second tier. The wihana of Heramba is the lion; but
an unusual image of Heramba was found at Bhatgaon, dated a.p. 1695,? which has
a rat instead of a lion for vikana, while a bronze image now in the Museum fur
Vélkerkunde in Munich has a rat under one foot and a lion under the other.*

A Heramba form of Ganega found in Nepalese paintings conforms with various
Mahayana- Buddhist representations of deities, and was popular with the Brahmans
as well as with the Buddhists in Nepal. Ganapati is figured as usual with five heada,
of which the central head, as well as the body, arms, and legs, like the Buddhist
deity Avalokiteévara, is white.* The head to the right is blue; to the left, yellow;
above the central head, green; and the top head is red. On the forehead of each
head is a third eye, on both sides of which is the Saiva tilaka, that is, three horizontal
lines; and one head wears the five-leafed crown. He has twelve arms. The normal
left hand balances a bowl of cakes under the trunk, while the normal right grasps a
tuak-shaped radish, holding it as if about to put it in the place of the missing tusk.
All the other aymbols are Tantric. He steps to the right like the dharmapala of
Mahayana Buddhism, and under each foot is a rat.

Ganeéa has a more tranquil aspect in Nepal when represented as a drarapdla
guarding the entrance to Buddhist monasteries. He always has for vis-d-vis, to the
right, the Tibetan god Mahakala whose worship was brought into Nepal by the
leamed Bandhudatta, contemporary of Hsiian-tsang.® Although the ‘Great Black
One’ waa the most popular deity in Tibet, he never drew worshippers to any extent
in Nepal where his image is rarely met with except in this role of guardian of the
monasteries. ®

In north India, aa well as in Chinese Turkestan, Ganeda was never adopted as
dvarapala. ‘The entrance to the Buddhist monasteries was guarded by Mahakdla
to the right and the Yaksi Hariti to the left. Although Gancéa was thus replaced
by Hariti as guardian of vihdras in Chinese Turkestan, he waa, nevertheless, moat
popular in Khotaa.’ In fact, he drew worshippers in all the Buddhist templea in
northern Asia that had fallen under the influence of Mahiyana Buddhism.

Bronze tablets and painted wooden panels were discovered in the sand near the

that the cushion seems to be, in reality, another pair of legs.? In Japan, as will be

by the esoteric sects, which is looked upon as a fusion of the ‘double form’ called

His face is yellow in colour but his body, bare to the waist, is pink outlined in red.
This is contrary to the Indian Dvi-debaka, who, as seen above, haa the face red and
the body yellow. On some of the painted panela in Chinese Turkestan, however, he

upper left grasps what was probably the axe, while the normal left balances a
radish (white) at his breast. He wears many jewels: necklace, bracelets, and ankleta.

In the cave temples of Bazaklik there are several freecos in which Ganeda ia figured

object against his breast which may be the modaka. He wears many jewels, and
behind his head is a nimbus.”

The interesting feature of these frescos of Ganesa is that the elephant-face doea

of the nose and the end of the trunk somewhat resemblea the snout of a wild boar.
The image, however, seems to be Ganega rather than Vardha for two reasons: firat,

When lying under the foet of Mahakala he was usually represented without a
crown but wearing a complicated and ormate costume and many jewels. He had
rarely more than two arms and might hold a bowl in his right hand while his left
in in tarjani-mudra.

The Tibetans conceived a female aa well asa male form of Ganeéa. In the Pantheon
of the Techangicha Hutuktu there is reference not only to a single form of Ganapati
under the feet of Mahakdla but to a group where Wahdédla crushes an image of
Ganapati under each foot, one male and the other female.’ This is consistent with
the Tibetan conception of their deities, for as a dharmapdla was believed to be more
powerful when manifesting with his feminine counterpart or éakti, 90 would the
triumph of Mahakala over the Hindu demon be more complete with one foot
crushing the female and the other the male form of Ganapati. Tibet was not the
only country to conceive a female form of Ganeda, aa will be seen below.

Ganeéa, however, is not always represented completely prostrate when under the
feet of fighting deities. Buddhist statues from both Nepal and Tibet figure him in
two poses, one lying on hia left side with the body partially raised and leaning on the
left arm. In this position he is under the feet of a peaceful deity, while in the other
attitude where he ia half raised, half kneeling, there is a fierce deity towering
over him,

When in the former pose he is to be conceived as vanquished by the goddess
Aparajita, who, according to dhdranis, in one of her peaceful forms euch as Pita-
aparajitd should be represented two-armed and seated with one foot on Ganeda.?

According to certain sddhanas, however, she should be conceived standing with
her left foot on the left leg of Gayeda, in which pose she is called Ganapatisamakranta
or ‘she who tramples on Ganapati'.> Or she may have one foot posed on his left
hip and the other on his head ; but he is, however, never to he considered absolutely
vanquished even if represented completely prostrate, in which case he carries a
dagger (phurbu) in his right hand.‘

When in the half-kneeling pose he is under the feet of Vighnanteka, ‘Destroyer
of Obstacles’, and a powerful Nepalese Buddhist deity. The word tighna (obstacle)
when found in the Sddhanamala usually referred to Ganega, but it was used for
another deity in a Nepalese legend which explains how the Nepalese Buddbist
‘Destroyer of Obstacles’ was created, and runs as follows: A pandit from Odiyana
waa performing special rites on the bank of the river Bagmati near Katmandu, in
order to attain a certain stage of perfection or siddhi. Ganeda, wishing to prevent the
pious Buddhist from attaining siddii, put insurmountable obstacles in his way.
The pandit, unable to perform the required rites, invoked, in great distress, the .
Buddhist ‘ Destroyer of Obstacles’, who appeared in the fierce form of Vighndntaka,
and Ganeéa was overcome.

The Nepalese ‘Destroyer of Obstacles’ is imaged with aix or eight arms.5 Asa
rule, he holds in his upper right arm a sword and in hia upper left the buckler.

Thus the Nepalese Buddhist ‘Destroyer of Obstacles’ is figured bringing the

Bhattacharyya refers to the intoresting fact that whereas the Trimarti, that is,
Brahma, Vignu, and Siva accompanied by Indra, are to be found grouped under the
feet of Marici or of Trailokyavijaya, Ganeda is always alone when under the feet
of a fighting Buddhist deity; doubtless because as ‘Creator of Obstacles’ he was
believed to be the greater danger to the successful propagation of Buddhism.

Although he is also to be found in a Tibetan group in the same position as in the
above Nepalese images, Ganeéa was conceived in Tibet as overcome and conquered
by the Tibetan god Mahakala. He is represented kneeling on the lotus throne, his
lega stretched to the right and the left on an angle with hia body.’ Mahakala steps
to the right with his fect posed on the feet of Ganeéa, who is two-armed, holding
with his left hand the usual bowl and grasping in his uplifted right his broken tusk.
He is one-headed and without a crown.

Ganeda, is found in Tibet under the feet of another deity who is none other than
the great Dhyani-Bodhisattva, Majijusri, in his Tantric form designated as ‘ Black’
Mafijuéri.? The Tibetan god of Wisdom in this manifestation is represented fierce
in expression and should have the third eye. Above his forehead is a simple crown,
and behind it, his upstanding hair is drawn into a high chignon in which is seated
his Dhyani-Buddha, Akshobhya. His right hand holds the sword above his head
in the same attitude as in his mild forms, while his left grasps what seems to be a
coiled serpent.? Griinwedel, describing his illustration of the ‘Black’ Maiijuéri,
calls the object in his left hand a ‘lotus with coiled stem’. What seems, in the bronze
image, to be the head of a serpent may, therefore, be a lotus-bud; or is he carry-
ing the celestial creeper, the stem of which is also represented as coiled? At
any rate, he has serpents for bracelets and ankleta, and a serpent is tied across his
breast. He steps to the right with his foot on the head of Ganeda, but unlike
the other groups described above his left foot is posed directly on the lotus
dsana.

Under the feet of Mafjugri, Ganeéa lies on his back with his head turned to the
right. There are no symbols in his hands; but a detail of interest is that, unlike the
above groups, Ganega is represented unmistakably as a Hindu deity and not as a
demon, for be wears the jatd-mukula of Siva. Since Mafjuéri is looked upon as
‘Lord of Wisdom’ by the Mahayana Buddhists, and Gancéa aa ‘God of Wisdom’

CHAPTER V
GANESA IN BURMA, SIAM, INDO-CHINA
TS gradual occupation of Indo-China by Hindu emigrants began at a very
early date, poasibly in the early centuries preceding our era.1 The peaceful
invaders came by sea or overland via Burma; and the slow process of Indianization
brought with it the adoption of Brahmanism as well as Buddhism, and the worship
of Indian gods.

As innumerable small images of Ganega have been found in Burma, Siam, and
west Indo-China which are Hindu in type, it is thought that they were probably
brought to these countries by Indian tradera who carried them along with their
merchandise in order to propitiate the ‘Remover of Obstacles’ and thus secure
success in their commercial enterprises. Strangely enough, no small Hindu images
of this type have been found in east Indo-China although Saiviem was flourishing
there as early as in Cambodia. From this it seems probable that the worship of
Ganeda was introduced into Champa by Hindu devotees or Brahman priests rather
than by Indian traders.

Burma was the firat éape in the eastward exodus of the Hindus from north-east
India. Crossing the Bay of Bengal, they found the population of the deltaic region
of lower Burma, which was inhabited by the Mén, given over to a form of Hinayanist
Buddhism hostile to the cult of gods and goddesses. The trader-colonists succeeded,
however, in setting up their deities at an carly date, if we judge from the images of
Vignu, Brahma, Ganeda, and other Hindu gods, dating from the sixth and seventh
centuries, that have been found in lower Burma.? Upper Burma, on the contrary,
was Mahayanist, and Tantric Mahaydnism, with its pantheon of gods and goddesses,
was in favour as late aa the eleventh century. When Brahmanism spread to north
Burma, a more fertile field was thus found for the introduction of the Hindu gods;
and while in lower Burma no Brahman deity was allowed in a Buddhist temple, in
upper Burma, as seen in the Shwesandaw pagoda at Pagan, for example, Ganega in
company with other Hindu gods waa placed in guard of the ancient Buddhist shrine.

Although statues of Ganega have been found in upper and lower Burma, his popu-
larity was greatest with the commercial population in the deltaic region, where
great numbers of small images of the type referred to above have been discovered,
and where he was known and worshipped as Mahdpienne.? None of the representa-
tions of Ganeéa in Burma, however, are of interest either from the point of view of
art or of iconography, being crude in execution and of the well-known Hindu types;
but a most remarkable sfela was discovered in the ruins of a Brahmanic temple at
Pagan‘ which will be described below when comparing it with a Khmer and with
a Cham statue, all three of which have the eame unusual features.

Pushing farther eastward, the indian travellers found the Mén also established
" According to Sir Charles Eliot, probably in » Idem, p. 60.

in south Siam on the banks of the Menam. It is not known what forra of Buddhism
they practised ; but as the Hindu emigrante were of superior culture, the process of
Indianization was more rapid than in Burma, From the sixth to the eighth centuries
they exerted a strong influence on the statuary art of the Mon; and Grousset looks
upon this period as 4 transition stage between the Gupta art of India and the pre-
Angkor art of Cambodia.’

In Siam, Ganega waa aa popular with the Buddhists as with the Brahmans. His
image, however, waa never allowed within the precincta of the Buddhist temples,
but wus tolerated within the temple grounds. Unfortunately no statue of any
interest has been found in south Siam. We only know of his popularity from the many
small images referred to above, which were probably copied and recopied by native
artisans. Not until the Ayuthian period in central Siam is a Siamese representation
of Ganeda to be found which is worthy of study.

Toward the ninth century, the Mon moved farther north and founded, in central
Siam, a second state, the kingdom of Haripufjaya. Unlike the kingdom of south
Siam, they resisted the Khmer; but in the thirteenth century the Mon were over-
powered by warriors from the north, the Thal, and became a part of the new state
with ita capital at Ayuthia. The early art of Ayuthia, although influenced by the
Indian culture of the conquered Mon, developed characteristics of ita own, as will
be seen by a very fine example of Ayuthian statuary art which was formerly in the
private collection of H.R.H. Prince Damrong.?

Ganeéga ia represented seated in the Mahdrajalila pose with the right knee uplifted,
while the left is bent before him on the deana. The head, well placed on the shoulders,
ia modelled with considerable skill, and the trunk is of unusual suppleness. The
eyes, as in India, are round and staring. The head is covered by a karanda-muluta,
tiara-shaped in front, behind which rises a spiral-shaped usnisa ending in a point
and somewhat resembling the spiral head-dress of a four-headed Ganeéa described
below.3 ‘The body is erect and, like the Khmer statues in general, ia not obese but is
normally modelled. He is bare to the waist and has a serpent over his shoulder in
place of the Brahmanical thread.

One of the specially interesting features of this Ayuthian bronze is the arrange-
ment of the arma, which, aa far as the author knows, is unique. From the shoulders
to the elbow, there is only one arm; but at the elbow, the arm branches into two,
somewhat like the images of Hindu gods in the Orissa districts of India. Two of the
hands are lifted on a level with the shoulders, holding a lasso and an attribute
difficult to determine. The wrist of the normal hand is posed on the knee; but the
hand is raised in what may possibly be a mudrd. The normal right, which resta on
the upraised knee, holds the broken tusk. The Ayuthian sculptor, however, was
apparently not versed in Hindu iconography, for the image is possessed of both
tusks; and the broken-tusk attribute is thus without raison d’¢tre.

Crouching under his uplifted right leg is his réhana, the rat, which we shall not
come across cither in Cham or in Khmer images of Ganesa; and shall rarely meet

In the Brahmanic temple at Bangkok there is a remarkable bronze statue of

represents Ganega as a scribe; but ia he to be looked upon as scribe to Vydsa or to
his father, Siva? Either, or both, legends might have penetrated into Cambodia.

The Mahabadrata waa known in Cambodia ax early as the sixth century; but the
north India recension of the epic poem with the introduction containing the legend
of Ganega as amanuensis to Vydsa could not have penetrated into Cambodia until
a later date. It is interesting to note that none of the images of Ganedga as acribe,
known to us, are prior to the eleventh century; and that it was in the eleventh cen-
tury that the kingdom of Dvaravatt, founded by the Indianized Mon, was united
to the Khmer empire. May it be inferred from this that the Mon, who, with their
Indian culture, were probably acquainted with the north recension of the #fahd-
bharata, introduced the legend into Cambodia ?

If, on the other hand, the representations of Ganesa holding the etylus were
inspired by the sacred Gayatri-tantra,? wherein Ganesa is referred to as writing down
the Tantras to the dictation of his father Siva, the legend would have penetrated
into Cambodia at an earlier date. This important Saiva tantra was perhaps known
in Cambodia in the ninth century, when Saivism was not only at ita height but in
great favour with the Khmer dynasty. During the reign of Jayavarman II, who
came to the throne in 4.p. 802, Tantric texta were introduced into Cambodia by
Hiranyadama, a Hindu Brahman priest well versed in the cult of the Siva-linga.
Unfortunately there is no document to prove the one or the other theory, or even
that the legend was known in Cambodia; but as neither the Buddhist nor the
Brahmanic statuary art was based on pure fantasy, it is safe to say that this unusual
representation of Ganeéa as ecribe was inspired by one of these two legends.

An old Siamese manuscript, now in the library of the Ecole Francaise d’Extréme
Orient at Hanoi, contains six images in outline of Ganeéa, of which three® are of
special interest. They are extremely ornate and stand with the knees bent and far
apart, whereas the heels almost touch each other. Two of the figures are called
Vighneévara and are of intereat because one of them is standing on a rat and the other
on a tortoise. The third Vindyaka is a curious and unique representation of which
the author has been unable to find an explanation. He is called Koficananeévara
and has three crowned heads, and wears an ornate sampot and many jewels. He
has six arms. The hands carry: at the right, a small image of an elephant with

The cult of Siva penetrated into Cambodia from the Funan, where Brahmanism

Some of these images are Hindu in type or show Indian influence; but the Khmer

The ‘borrowing’, as it were, of Buddhist features for the representations of a
Hindu deity is not inconceivable. Buddhism was introduced into Indo-China
practically at the same time as Brahmanism and, by the fifth century, was also
drawing many followers in Champa.

As the two religions were practised side by side, it is not surprising to find a Hindu
image of Ganega reflecting Buddhist iconography, as, for instance, in the position
of the legs, where we find the deity in the characteristic pose of the Buddha or
padmdsana, that is, legs locked with the aoles of both feet apparent.! Another
interesting feature of thia Cham statue is a protuberance in the middle of the forehead.*

Certain esoteric Buddhist sects in India attempted to explain the wsnisa or pro-
tuberance on the head of the Buddha as being the seat of his divine mind. We have
seen above? in the legend from the Vardha-purdya that Ganesa was ‘mind-born’,
having been produced from a brilliant flame emanating from the forehead of Siva.
From this, certain esoteric Hindu sects were led to believe that Ganesa was the
personification of Siva’s divine manas or mind, It therefore does not seem improb-
able that this protuberance on the forehead of Ganega was meant to symbolize the
seat of the divine mind of Siva. At any rate, this hypothesis may be accepted
until a better one be found to explain the presence of this unusual feature on the
Cham statue of Ganega.

The curiously shaped head of the above figure gives the impression of an elephant-
face mask behind which is the normal head, especially as the ears seem to be attached
to the normal head and not to the elephant face. In Indian miniatures and paintings
of Ganeéa‘ this same idea, referred to above, is shown, since the elephant-face ia
painted red while the ears and neck are white or pink.

In the case of thia image, the impression of a mask behind which is the normal
head is even greater, for there are two foreheads, one above the other. On the lower
forehead, that is, on that of the elephant-face mask, is the protuberance, while on
the upper forehead, slightly farther back (which belongs to the normal head), is the
third eye. The round staring eyes are on the elephant-face. The normal head is
covered by a closely braided head-dress held in place by a flat tiara-shaped band
and, on the top of the head, is the lotus rosace referred to above. Like most of the
cated images of Ganesa in Indo-China, the hands rest on the knees holding the bowl
and the tusk. :

There are two other representations of Ganega which also seem to indicate atrong
Buddhist influence. One ia a small Khmer image now in the Museum fir Vélker-
kunde in Munich. The other is the Burmese stela referred to above.

an elephant-face mask, behind which is the normal head. In this case he wears an

Stelas have been found both in Cambodia and Champa which date as far back as

Inscriptions have been found in Indo-China in which Ganeéa is either included in a
list of deities or ia mentioned as accompanying Siva. It is interesting to note that
in the inscriptions both in Champa and in Cambodia, when Ganeda is referred to in
a group of gods auch as at Hod-que,” and at Mi-so’n,? he is called Ganeda, whereas,
when mentioned as attendant on Siva, especially in his liaga form, such as at Po

would seem from this that under the epithet of ‘Sri Vindyaka” he was looked upon
as the more important deity, especially so, since in an inscription at Mi-so’n there
is mention of a Sri-Vindyaka-Vijaya.*

In the region of Kompong Thom an inscription of the ninth century was dis-

interest because it refera to Ganeda as an independent deity and locally important,
and accentuates the tradition which followed him from India to Japan, of being
worshipped in connexion with Mountains.

CHAPTER VI
GANESA IN JAVA, BALI, BORNEO
Te are no ancient records either in India or Java which throw light on the
early exodus of the Hindus across the seas to the Malay Archipelago, carrying
with them their culture and religion and imposing the worship of their gods.

Java may have been known to the Hindus, nevertheless, at a very early date, for
the Sanskrit name ' Yara-defpa’, found in the ancient texts of the Ramayana, refers,
it is believed, to the ‘island of Java’! The word occurs in the text of the epic poem,
after the account of the abduction of Sita by the ten-headed god Ravana, when
Hanuman, chief of the monkeys, ordera them to seek for the wife of Rama; and
among other places he suggeats that they look for her in ‘ Yava-dvipa’.

It is not known at what time the Hindus actually established themselvea in Java,
but the Buddhist traveller Fa-hsien on hia way back to China from India in the year
A.D. 414 stopped at *Yava-dvipa’ and describes the island as peopled by ‘heretics
and Brahmana’.? From this, it is evident that Brahmanism was flourishing there
as early as the fifth century; but no inscription teatifies to Brahmanic rites until the
eighth century, when a stone inscription dated «.p. 732? found at Chantal gives an
account of the consecration of a lige, with invocationa to ‘Siva, Brahma, and
Vignu’. The fact that Siva is mentioned first in the Trimarti shows that he took
precedence over Brahma and testifies to his popularity in Java, even were it not
plainly evident from the many sanctuaries dedicated to the Lord of the Trident
which were found on the island.

It is thus not surprising that statucs of Ganega, son of Siva, should be met with
in great numbers throughout the Malay Archipelago. There is, however, no evidence
of an ancient Ganedéa cult, no temples ecem to have been dedicated to him alone;
but his images are sometimes to be found, as in the rock-cut temples of India, in a
niche beside the Siva sanctuaries.

In Java as in India, no trausitional form of Gancda has as yet been met with
unless the roughly carved elephant-headed deity discovered in west Java‘ and a
amali bronze image now in the British Museum® be accepted as primitive concep-
tions of the Elephant-faced god.

According to Krom,* the crude stone sculpture may be only an unfinished attempt
to produce an image of Ganedga; but the representation is so primitive even in its
unfinished state that one is tempted to give it a very carly date.

The figure is two-armed; the legs are missing, and there is no head-dress; but
above the forehead ia incised an uneven line evidently to indicate the hair. The one
eye is not round and staring as in the early Indian forms but elongated, and, although
traced by a simple incision, resemblea a human eye. The ears are large, human in

form, and lic flat against the head. The trunk is long and hangs straight, being only

coiled to the left at the end. The hands, which are posed on the breast touching the

trunk, are not human hands, for the fingers are all of the same length and unfor-
tunately hold no symbole.

The small bronze image in the British Museum although crude in ite conception,
has more detail than the stone statue. Above the forehead is a tiara-shaped crown,
behind which the hair hangs straight at the back; and he wears a necklace and
bracelets. The eyes are round and staring as in the Indian images, and the ears
are small and human in shape. The trunk is short and curved with the end coiled
underneath,! which is most unusual. The hands, which are more human in aspect
than those of the stone image, are also posed on the breast but lower down; and the
left hand may have supported the bowl. A small but later bronze figure belong-
ing to M. Coedés? shows these same characteristics, that is, the trunk hanging
straight while the two hands at the breast are against the trunk as if they were
supporting it.

An interesting point about these two bronze images ia that they have the three
characteristics of practically all of the Javanese representations of Ganeéa: first,
that, unlike the Indian conception when, if seated, the legs are wide apart, the
obese torso thus touching the ground, the early Javanese Ganeéa sits erect with
the legs bent beneath him, thus supporting the torso. Second, that the soles
of the feet are touching, which is a position never found outside of the Malay
Archipelago. The third characteristic of the early Javanese statues is that, with few
exceptions, the trunk hangs straight and may be coiled to the left. towards the end.
This characteristic may also be found in Indo-China,’ but in India it is only met
with when the trunk is balancing the modaka in its coiled end.*

From these two crude images to the remarkable stone figure found on the Dieng
plateau which is believed to be the most ancient representation of Ganeéa in Java®
there is a wide gap. It is to he hoped that one day the transitional forms will be
found which led up to this representation of Ganeéa which has never been surpassed
in any country. At first glance, it seemus to be the classic, four-armed image known
to us in India; but on closer examination it is found that its conception is not
purely Indian ; besides which, the Indo-Javanese conceived a Ganeéa of great dignity,
for he is seated erect with hia head well placed on his shoulders. The special position
of the feet, with the soles touching, gives the same impression of poise as do the
locked legs of the deities of Mahayana Buddhism.

The image from the Dieng plateau is represented as four-armed, his normal hands
posed on his knees, the right holding his broken tusk, the left the bowl, while the
upper arms carry the axe and rosary. His trunk hangs atraight and turns to the
left on a level with the bow]. The elephant-head is remarkably true to life with no
attempt at giving it a human aspect. like we find in later images. The body is
a normal human body without being exaggerated in detail as are the Hindu images.

throne supported by two genii. He is figured practically the same aa in the
above statues and his eclephant-face is also extraordinarily true to life; but he
differs in that he has the third eye.’ Curiously enough, instead of a mukuta
he has on the top of his head a flattened lotus flower, the petals of which are
turned backward. The only other example of Ganeéa with a lotus head-ornament
known to the author is the Cham statue in the Tourane Museum referred to above.”
Another unusual feature of this image is that the bracelets and anklets are made
of serpenta.

Ina niche in the ruins of a temple near Deasa Woedjel, a fine statue of Ganega was
found which is now in Leyden. He is represented four-armed carrying the usual
symbols and wearing a high jafd-mukufa on his head. The eyes, although wide apart,
are human eyes; but the most interesting feature of the statue is that the body,
inatead of being erect in pose, as in the earlier images, is heavy and ramassé, a
characteristic of practically all of the later seated representations of Ganega in Java.
Tt is curious to note that the more ornate the statue, the lesa erect is the pose and
the more sunken is the head between the shoulders.

One of the moat remarkable examples of this ramassé type is the celebrated
Tjandi Singasari statue, also now in Leyden.? Unlike the other images of Ganeda
in Java, the solea of the feet do not touch and the right knee is raised in almost the
mahardajalila pose. He has four arms and holds the usual symbols, His eyes are
small, round, and staring, and he wears bracelets and anklets and a snake girdle.
The head falls so low on the breast that the shoulders are alinost on # line with the
mukufa. ‘The striking feature of the Singasari image is the skull omamenta on the
karanda-mukula and the car-rings; while, instead of the usual padmdsana, he is
seated on a throne ornamented with a row of skulls.

The use of the skull ornaments on the representations of Ganega is a purely
Javanese conception and is practically never met with outside of the Malay Archi-
pelago. Siva, as has been seen above, when manifested as Bhairava, wears a garland
of skulla a3 well aa skulls in his jafd-mukufa. In a country where this form of the
Lord of the Trident was popular, it should not be surprising to find the skull orna-
ments used for his son. In fact, one may even find in the Jatd-mukuta worn by
Ganesa, and undemeath a skull ornament, the crescent moon of Siva; and an
interesting Suiva form of Ganesa was found near Ngadirejo, in a temple dedicated
to Siva and Parvati, where he is represented holding a small statue of Nandi,
Siva's vdhana.

A still more exaggerated form of the ramassé type of Ganeéa is found in the statue
at Bara,’ which is both Indo-Javanese and Indonesian? in atyle.

This atone image found near Djembe is best known as the ‘Ganeda of Bara’. Tho
figure is eeated ; and the head which is out of all proportion with the body, almost
covers the torso. The cyes are large and human in shape with heavy eyebrows. The
four arms hold the usual symbols and the legs are bent underneath him with the
roles of the feet touching. He weara an ornate karanda-mukuta and car-rings,
braceleta, and ankleta. Embroidered bands hang over the bent legs partially cover-
ing the dsana, which is decorated with a row of skulls in front, while at the back
there is an inscription dated 1239.°

The unusual! feature of the Bara Ganeda is that while the front is of Indo-Javanese
style, the back is elaborately carved in Indonesian manner, with the head of a
goggle-eyed monster such as are placed in Java on either side of the entrances to the
temples to guard against evil spirita’ These yaksa guardians with tusk-like fangs
are also to be met with in Indo-China.

The Bara Ganeda was not found in a niche near a Siva sanctuary. As a matter of
fact, comparatively few of his representations in Java have been found in niches;
and Stutterheim attributes this fact to the quantity of statues of Ganeéa that have
been discovered which far outnumber those of any other deity, even Siva. He is
of the opinion, judging from their having been met with ea a rule in isolated spots,
that Ganeda was revered by travellers in Java as ‘Remover of Obstacles’ in their
perilous voyages ; and this seems to be proved by the fact that his images have been
found in places of danger such as steep slopes, river crossings, &c.

In India, especially in south India, it has been seen that along the highroads, and
particularly where two roads crossed, there was invariably a crude image of Ganeéa
or a formless stone (svayambhi-mirti) symbolizing him, and the traveller, by
making a humble offering, might thus become immune from all danger. In Kashmir
his svayambhi-mirtis are sometimes placed in river beds.

In Java the rivers were looked upon, according to Stutterheim, as mystic boun-
daries fraught with danger and hazardous to cross without proper magic protection.*
As the Bara Ganeéa waa found at the dangerous passage of the river Brantas, it i
more than probable that it was placed in this isolated spot for a special purpose.
This, however, does not explain the presence of the Indonesian guardian-monster
head carved at the back of the statue.

We have seen above that Ganesa, both in Nepal and Tibet, was entrusted with
guarding the entrance to temples and mhdras against evil spirits by putting obstacles
in their way. May not this tradition have reached Java? The patron goddess of
Nepal, Guhycévari, as will be seen below, was probably known to the Javancse.

If the sculptor of the Bara Ganega conceived the idea of creating an image which,
when placed at the passage of a river, could not only protect the traveller, if properly
propitiated, by removing obstacles on a dangerous crossing, but guard the passage

Unlike Siam and Indo-China, Java produced comparatively few bronze images of
Ganega, but they surpass the stone sculptures both in conception and presentation.

In the Museum fiir Volkerkunde in Munich there ia a very fine Javanese bronze
statue of a seated, four-armed Ganega.' The head, especially the ears and trunk,
is admirably modelled. His karanda-mukufa is omamented with skulls and his
dsana is decorated with two rows of skulls, which is unusual. In his upper right
band he holds a serpent, the body of which crosses his breast like a Brahmanical
thread, while in his upper left hand he grasps a hoop-shaped garland. In each of his
normal hands, posed on the knees, he holds a bowl, a feature which is rarely met
with, except in standing images. The soles of his feet touch, but they are not normal
feet as all the toes are of the same length. From his belt hang bands which fall over
his legs, but like all the early images in Java, he is represented nude and sitting
very erect.

The most beautiful of the bronze statues of Ganeéa is now in Leyden.” He is
figured seated on a throne which, at the back, forms a nimbus; and above him is the
royal parasol. The throne is supported by elephants, and between them is the rat,
his mount, which we meet with for the first time in Java. As far as the author knows,
this bronze image is the only Javanese representation of Ganeés accompanied by
his wihana.

The Elephant-faced god is seated on a round cushion in the mahdrdjalila pose,
but with this difference from the usual attitude, in that the knee is held uplifted by
a searf tied around the body from the left hip, an ancient custom of Indian ascetics
and of high-caste Hindus.

The attitude with the ecarf is met with in the ancient Indian school such as at
Sanchi and in other Buddhist aculptures in India; and it is not unknown in Java,
where, in the Buddhist bae-reliefe at Boro-Budur, there are two fine examples. One
is that of the father of the prince Sudhana in indolent repose in his palace ;> and
the other is that of the Bodhisattva Siddbartha reclining in his chariot under the
royal parasol, after having met with an aged person for the first time.‘

Although the mahdrdjalila pose with the searf may be found not infrequently in
stone sculptures, it is seldom met with in bronze statues, Besides this Javanese
image of Ganesa, there is only one other example known to the author, that ofa
Tibetan bronze statue of Avalokitedvara, who is seated with the right knee uplifted
and held in place by a scarf tied around the body from the left bip and knotted at
the knee.®

It seems incongruous to find this attitude adopted for a Brahmanic god, which, in
Buddhist iconography, as depicted in the Javanese Buddhist sculptures, is used for
a deity of importance such as a Bodbisattva. We have scen above many examples

Brahmanic rites are recorded as being officiated by Brahmanic priests. Thus it is not
impossible that the statue of Ganesa found in the grotto at Kombeng is one of the
oldest in the Malay Archipelago.'

In Bali, on the contrary, no record has been found to throw light on the early
adoption of Brahmanism in the island; but according to Chinese records,” Bali waa
already a religious centre in the Middle Ages resisting Moslem influence and, in fact,
offering a refuge from all religious persecutions. ,

Before the eighth century the Balinese worshipped Brahma and Visnu and the
group of five deities known to us in sonth India: Siva, Durga (Parvati), Visnu,
Surya, and Ganesa, During the eighth and ninth centuries the Saiva cult was
especially flourishing in Bali ;3 and Siva, accompanied by Durga and Ganeéa, as well
as by his mount, Nandi, the bull, was in great favour, if one judges from the number
of temples dedicated to him alone. When represented in this group, he was often
in Wage form.

It is probable that Ganeda, aa one of the group of five deities, was not without his
share of worshippers; and he may also have drawn a certain number of adorers
when accompanying Siva; but no evidence of an actual Ganedga cult has been found
which would justify the great number of his images found in Bali.

Asa rule, Ganesa is represented standing, and although his statues have not been
found in Bali, as in Java, at river crossings or other places of danger, he may never-
theless have been looked upon as Protector against Evil Spirits, His images are
often met with in niches in the Gandis, where his sapect is singularly more Chinese
than Indian or Javanese. This is not surprising, since the Chinese were settled on
the island possibly as carly as the Hindus,‘ at any rate, quite early enough to stamp
their influence on the art of Bali.

When standing, especially in tjandi niches, the Balinese Ganesa in represented
short and thick-set, with the head often disproportionately large and wearing a
huge and complicated crown, in graduated tiers of leaf-motives with an ornament
in the centre. He usually has the third eye and his normal eyes are large, often
goggled, and sometimes round and staring.

He is bare to the waist, but below the jewelled girdle hang scarves draped around
the hips with an omate end falling in front to the ground. Across his breast, like a
Brahmanical thread, is a serpent with the head at the left shoulder, A feature of the
trunk which is only found in Bali is that toward the end it is sometimes snake-like,
being carved with the same scale motifs as the aerpent hanging from the left shoulder.®

Another feature of this standing form which has not as yet been met with ia that
Ganeéa has a anake and sometimes two, coiled around his ankles. We have scen that
in Nepal, Ganeda may hold above his head, with his upper arms, the eerpent Sega,
while in Java he may have serpents for ommaments; but the symbolic meaning of
the serpents coiled around his feet, which is evidently based on tradition since eo
often repeated, escapes, thus far, all explanation.

The ecated images of the Balinese Ganega are repreecnted as a rule with the aoles

of Yeh Pocloe is two-armed with the hands posed on the knees as in Indo-China.

Tn the village of Bretit a small stone figure of Ganeda was found which is of
interest because of the head-dress, for on the top of the head, behind the tiara-
sbaped crown, is a large flattened lotus-flower such aa we have already met with in
Java! and have found in Indo-China.?

In the Malay Archipelago as well as in Indo-China, it was an ancient custom to
make posthumous statues of kings and queens, imaging them as gods and goddesses
and usually representing them standing in groupe. Stutterheim has observed the
almost invariable presence of Ganesa to the extreme right of the Balinese groups of
deified personages, who were represented standing on lotus-Howers and wearing the
same elaborate mukutas as Ganeéa when he is figured in tjandi nichea or seated on &
lotus throne.

Stutterheim believes that his worship was in eome way drawn into the ceremonies
of deifying the statues of kings and queens. As these images were made in com-
memoration after the death of the royal personages, it is not impossible that
Ganeéa, who was undoubtedly worshipped during their lifetime as ‘Remover of
Obstaclea’, was placed at the right of cach group in order, if properly propitiated, to
remove all ohstaclea from their patha after death.

A statue found in Djembaran seems to show that Ganeéa had eome euch tradition
in south Bali? He is seated in the Javanese attitude, the right hand holding a torch,
the left a bowl. The trunk is short and coiled underneath as in the ancient Javancee
bronze now in the British Museum.* The head-dress is Indonesian, but the detail of
apecial interest is that the throne is surrounded by flames. Bencath the feet is an
inscription which is tranaliterated prds (7).

Although the statue is somewhat later than thoee described above, it is none the
leas possible that the use of the flame-motive was based on an ancient tradition
executed, as far aa is now known, for the first time in atone. The sculptor of gods
and goddeasea never added either symbols or other elements of pure fantasy until
towards the seventeenth or eighteenth century; and if the aymbolic meaning of
eome of the details is lost to ua, it is none the less certain that their presence was
authorized by religious canon or local legend.

Such being the case, may not the form of Ganeéa holding a torch and seated on
a throne ewrounded by flames be considered as throwing light on his role when
accompanying the group of deified kings and queens? Was he looked upon ss
taking charge of the royal personages after death like the Shingon Fudo, who is
also represented surrounded by flames? At any rate, we have come upon & con-
ception of Ganeéa which has been met with in no other country.

interest, now in the Kern Institute at Leyden,’ was discovered in a village called

Originally he was four-armed, but only one arm remaina, the hand of which rests

wears a triangular collar, the point falling between the breasts and held in place by

As in most of the images of Ganega found in the Malay Archipelago, the head is
the chief interest, and in thia bronze it is especially so, since it is unlike any that we
have yet met with. The trunk, which is short, turns directly upward and rises to a
level with the mukuta.

Another feature which is rarely met with in the Malay Archipelago is the pose of
the feet. He is not seated with the soles of the feet touching but is in the Indian
attitude of locked lege.

Since neither the raised trunk nor the pose of locked legs, nor even the mukuta,
indicate either Javanese or Balinese origin, it seems probable that this small
bronze image of Ganesa, although found in Bali, came originally from China or
Indo-China.

CHAPTER VII
GANESA IN CHINA
I: is not known at what epoch or by which route or routes the cult of Ganesa was
first introduced into China, and a still greater problem presents itself when
attempting to account for the double form of Ganeéa worshipped by the Chinese
which, outside of China and Japan, waa totally unknown,

‘There were two channels by which his cult might have reached China. The first,
and probably the earliest, was by the overland route either via Chinese Turkestan
or passing through Nepal and Tibet. His worship waa certainly brought to China
either by Indian pandits! or by Chinese Buddhist monks returning from their pil-
grimages to the Buddhist shrines of India, carrying back with them the tencts of the
Yogacara or Tantric echool favourable to the mystic cult of Ganeéa as conceived
by the Chinese.

No difficulty presente itself in tracing the second route, for precise records attest
to the voyages of Buddhist monks who crossed the seas from India to China, where
they introduced the Tantric texte and practices as well as the Mahayana doctrine
of the mandalas of the Two Parts: the Vajra-dhatu and the Garbha-dhatu, mystic
diagrame wherein Vindyaka had his allotted place.

Ganega was known to the Chinese as well aa to the Japanese under two
aspecta: Vinayaka, the single form, and Kuan-shi-t'ien (Kangi-ten), the double
form. The single form was similar to that found in practically all Buddhist
countries, that is, clephant-faced with two arms, and was usually cross-legged.
The double form, which, as we have said above, was totally unknown outside
of China and Japan, was represented by two elephant-faced deities standing vis
4 via, interlaced.

Unfortunately there are only two ancient representations of Ganeéa known to us
in China. One is a fresco on the wall of a grotto-temple at Tun-huang, while the
other is a stone bas-relief in the rock-cut temple at Kung-hsien. According to Paul
Pelliot, the frescos date from the first half of the sixth century, which would make
them contemporary with the stone sculptures of Kung-hsien. René Grousset traces
in the frescos at Tun-huang the progressive adaptation by the Chinese of Gan-
dharian, Gupta, and Iranian models ;? but were these purely Hindu frescos executed
by Buddhist Chinese, by Indian artists, or by both?

At this period, the finest frescos at Ajanta were being painted, and among the
ateady stream of Buddhist pilgrims from China to India were assuredly Chinese
artista who, like the illustrious Buddhist traveller Hetian-tsang, visited Ajanta and
returned to China via Tun-huang. While at Ajanta the gradual development of
mural painting may be traced which culminated in the masterpieces in Cave I, at
Tun-huang, although various influences are apparent, the frescos seem to have
been executed by sure and experienced hands. They were, however, apparently not

dy. p. 73. 2 H. de TE.-0., tome i. p. 316.

--- Page 92 ---
68 GANESA IN CHINA
creations like at Ajanta, but give the impression of having been copied from memory
from Hindu paintings which unfortunately are lost to us.

Above and on both sides of the Buddhist statues, which are of the Wei dynasty,
are the freacos which, eapecially in Cave N, No. 120, portray only Hindu deities:
Strya on his horse-drawn chariot, Candra on a chariot drawn by swans, the
Navagrahas in a frieze; while below is the god of Love, Kama, beside whom, on a
slightly lower level, is Ganega ecated in Maharajalila pose.

If we carefully study this representation of Ganesa, it is surprising to find details
of iconography, which apparently indicate a later period than Wei; and it seems
essential that we should note these points when describing the remarkable repre-
sentation of Vindyaka at Tun-huang.

We have referred above to a painting of Ganeda on a panel found in Chinese
Turkestan at Endere, which is said to be of the eighth century. If we compare this
representation of Vinayaka with that of Tun-huang, it will be found that there is a
resemblance between the two which scems inexplicable. For instance, let us study
the upper part of the head of the Tun-huang Ganega and compare it with that of his
representation at Endere. The head is presented three-quarters aa was usual in
Chinese Turkestan. The outline of the left eye, the contour of the bridge of the nose
from which the trunk develops, the difficult drawing of the right cye, are so similar
that one has the impression that if they were not drawn by the same hand, they
were executed by artists of the same school. The left arm of Ganeéa at Tun-huang
holds the long narrow white tusk at the breast exactly as does the Ganesa at Endere
the long narrow white radish. The turban head-dress seems to proclaim an Iranian
influence. The turban is drawn towards the front with the knots above the forehead
not only as on the Endere Ganeda but also aa on the painting of the well-known
Iranian Vajrapani, found in Chineae Turkestan, at Dandan-Uilig. The Ganeda at
Tun-huang does not wear the Persian tight-fitting trousers of the Vindyaka at
Endere but has on the Central Asian trousera of many folds. His trunk, although
turning to the right while that of the Ganeéa of Endere turns to the left, has the

same presentation, that is, it hangs straight for half ita length and then turns eide-
ways and upwards parallel with itself to a level with the shoulder. In the Tun-
huang Ganesa, it seems about to take a cake from the uplifted right hand on a line
with the shoulder. This pose of the trunk as well as that of the left hand at the breast
is unusual and up to the present time has not been found elsewhere.

Although the fresco of Ganeda at Tun-huang and the stone sculpture at Kung-
haien were apparently executed at the same epoch, they were assuredly not inspired
from the same source.

Undoubtedly the earlicat dated sculpture of an elephant-faced deity known to us
either in China or in India is the bas-relief in the Buddhist grotto-temple of Kung-
hsien! which, according to the inscription, was executed in a.p. 531. This Vinayaka
form of Ganeda is represented seated cross-legged with a lotus in his uplifted right
hand and a cinédmans in his left which is posed on his lap. According to the Chinese
characters % MP =E he is the ‘Spirit King of Elephants’; and is figured in

company with nine other inferior deities or ‘Spirit Kings’. This same group is
also represented on the base of a Buddhist monument dated a.p. 543! which was
formerly in the possession of M. Victor Goloubew and is now in Boaton.

We have seen above that in India, Ganeda often figures in groupe such aa the
Navagrahas and the Saptamatrkas; but in this Chinese group he is represented in
the company of nine deities unknown in India as @ group: the Spirit Kings of the
Nagas, of the Wind, of Pearls, of Fire, of Trees, of Mountains, of Fishes; and at one
end of the group are the ‘Spirit Kings’ of Lions, of Birds, and of Elephanta. It ia
perhaps only a coincidence that these last three—the lion, the bird, and the elephant
—should have become important symbols in Chinese Buddhiam, especially the lion
as mount of Samantabhadra and the elephant as rahana of the God of Wisdom,
Mafjuéri.

Tt seems incredible that we should find an image of the Elephant-faced god in
China dated as early as a.v. 531, when in India there is no representation of Ganeda
which can be attributed without question to the sixth century,’ although, aa we
have seen above, there is evidence of his having drawn worshippers in India at a
much earlier period.

The Chinese representation of Ganesa at Kung-hsien waa undoubtedly made from
a model brought to China either at the end of the fifth or the beginning of the sixth
century, for the elephant was little known in China at that epoch and it would thus
have been practically impossible for a Chinese sculptor to conceive a figure of
Ganeéa so closely resembling the Hinda conception.

Ae it was not the Chinese custom to adore the images of their deities, the pilgrims
coming to India from China muet have been struck with the Hindu practice of
worshipping the statues of their gods; and especially a0, with the adoration of
Hindu god having the face of an elephant, since in China the form of the elephant
was looked upon az a symbol of the conception of the Buddha.

It is not impossible, therefore, that Fa-haien, who retuned to Chins from India
in the beginning of the fifth century, brought back an imago of the elephant-faced
deity as an example of the strange Indian custom. We may equally suppose that
Yasogupta, Buddhanandi, and three other Buddhist prieste, who left Ceylon in
A.p, 460, passing through India and, aa stated in certain accounts, Tibet,” brought,
among the images they are said to have carried with them, one of the Elephant-faced
god, possibly to remove obetacles on their long and adventurous voyage.

His image might also have been carried to China by the Chinese travellers, Sung
Yin and Hui-sheng, who were sent to India at the beginning of the sixth century
by the empress Hou, a fervent Buddhist, with mission to bring back Buddhist texte.
They returned to China with an important collection of Mahayana texte, nine years
before the sculptures of Kung-hsien were executed, that is, in a.p. 522,

It is hardly probable at this epoch that his image should have been brought to
China with the idea of establishing a Ganeda cult; nor that his worship was set up
in China as early as Haitan-teang, in the last half of the seventh century, for in the

account of his travels in India, Hsiian-tsang makes no mention of Ganesa, although
he returned to China via Tun-huang,' nor did his Japanese disciple, Doshd, establish
a Gancéa cult in Japan, although he introduced the doctrine of Yoga or Mystic
Union.

Toward the end of the seventh century another Chinese Buddhist monk, I-ching,
returned to China after a long pilgrimage through India, but like Hatian-tsang, he
made no reference to Ganega or a Ganeéa cult in the records of his travels? although
in India as weil as in the Malay Archipelago, where he remained several years on his
return voyage, he must have become familiar with the image of the Elephant-faced
god and witnessed his popularity. It may therefore be safely inferred that the
Ganega cult was not introduced into China by I-ching.

Many other Chinese monks followed the example of Haiian-tsang by making
pilgrimages to India, and returned to China for the most part ardent adherents of
the Yogacara school. Of these, however, there seems to be only one, Heiian-chao,
whose personality was atrong enough to warrant even a surmise as to whether the
introduction of the double form of Gancéa into China, and possibly its esoteric cult,
were duc to him.

Heiian-chao started from China on a pilgrimage to India about a.p. 641. Under
the protection of the Chinese princess Wen-ch’eng who had become regent of Tibet
after the death of the Tibetan king Srong-tsan-gam-po, he was able to pass through
Tibet on his way to India where he remained for several yeara in the vthdra of
Nalanda.

The form of Buddhism taught at Nalanda in the time of Hsiian-chao, outwardly
Buddhist, was, in reality, saturated with Tantric thought and practices which up
to the seventh century had been handed down eecretly by the Buddhist priests and
revealed to the public only after complicated rites of initiation.

Even before the Buddha, Tantriam or the use of mystic formulae, sorcery, and
magic had been practised by the primitive peoples of north India; and according to
the Guhyasaméja-tanira, the Buddha himeelf was believed to have revealed the
mystic mantra, the Ganapati-hrdaya,? to his followers.

It was, however, not until the fourth century a.p. that the doctrine of the Tan-
trayana with ita mantras, dharanis, and mandalas was incorporated into Buddhism
and that one of the earliest Buddhist Tantras, the Guhyasamdja, was compiled.‘

In the same century another mystic school was founded, the Yogécara which con-
sisted in the practice of profound meditation (samadhi) whereby was obtained a state
of yoga or the ecatatic Union of the Individual with the Universal Spirit (Vairocana).

A pantheon of gods and goddeases was set up where the deities, for the most part,
were personifications of abstract ideas or of philosophical conceptions deitied—-such
as the five Dhyani-Buddhas. Strangely enough, several Hindu gods were taken over
and admitted to the Buddhist pantheon, and among these was Ganesa.

Up to the sixth century, the Yogacara echool, with its Hidden Doctrine, had
remained on a fairly spiritual plane, that ia to aay that yoga or communion with
Vairocana through meditation was practised principally by the intellectual. Gradu-
ally, however, the Hindu Tantras were incorporated into Buddhist literature and
the distorted interpretation of yoga led to Sakti-worship. The éakti waa looked upon
as the female energy of the god and was usually worshipped accompanied by the
male deity. It was, in fact, a form of ‘mother’ cult.

‘Tbe germ of Sakti-cultus waa held in the GuAyasamdja where, in a magic circle or
mandala the five Dhydni-Buddhas were deacribed as being accompanied by their
Saktis; but up to the sixth century the yoga aapect of a deity had rarely been
represented either in etatues or in paintings. The form adopted to represent the
Mystic Union was that of the deity seated with the Sakti beside him who was
usually imaged much amaller than the god. This yoga aspect was used not only for
the important deities but also for those not included in the Mahayana system, such
as Ganeda, for, as early aa the sixth century according to Coomaraawamy, he was
represented seated with the éaidi on hia left knee.?

About eleven years before the arrival of Hsiian-chao in Tibet, the Tibetan king
Srong-tsan-gam-po, who had been converted to Buddhism by his two consorts, a
Nepalese princess and the Chinese princess Wen-ch'eng, sent an emissary, by name
T’on-mi Sambhota,? to India to procure Buddhist texta. It is not known what
religious works he carried back with him to Tibet; but it ia safe to say that beside
Mahayana siitras and Yogacira texts he brought Tantras dealing with the Sakti-
cultus. On the other hand, it is also probable that strong Tantric influence was
tranamitted to Tibet through the Himalayan passes‘ from Nepal.

Hsiian-chao, arriving at the vihdra of Nalanda in the middle of the seventh cen-
tury with the intention of perfecting himself in Yoga, would have found a Sakti-cult
firmly established in India; and on returning through Nepal and Tibet, he would
inevitably have been struck by the difference between the Indian and the Tibetan
conceptions of the doctrine of Yoga.

Little is known of the Bon-po religion which, up to the advent of the princess
Wen-ch’eng, had been practised in Tibet; but it is probable, since the Tibetans were
a primitive people, that the Bon-po religion waa not only animist but Tantric, and
that Sakti-worship would thus have appealed to them more directly than would the
doctrines of the Yogacara schooi. It is undoubtedly also for this reason that the
yab-yum® form, or the gross conception of the Mystic Union, found favour in Tibet,
where the deities and their éaktis were represented interlaced.

Since Ganesa had been adopted by the Yogacarina as a Buddhist deity, his worship
was introduced by them into Tibet, where his yab-yum form would have been the most
popular. But how was the yab-yum form represented? As the Tibetans did not

adopt tbe Hindu representations of the gode with their saktis, it seems improbable
