Unfortunately, we know of no representation of Ganeda in the yab-yum attitude,
which is unquestionably Tibetan; but may not the double form known in China
and Japan be, in reality, the Tibetan yab-yum form? There are instances in Tibet
where the yum is figured almost the size of the yab and is imaged facing him.’ The
difficulty of presenting an elephant-headed god in yab-yum with his dakti who had
also the bead of an elephant, would necessitate a somewhat different arrangement of
the deities from the usual Tibetan yab-yum form. At any rate as the Tantric aspect
of Mystic Union was unknown in China and Japan either in sculpture or paintings,
the double form of Ganeéa was evidently due to outside influence. On the other
hand, since the interlaced forms as conceived in Tibet were not adopted outside
of Nepal, Mongolia, and ‘Tibet, may we not infer that the esoteric form of Kuan-
shi-t’ien found in China and Japan either came originally from these countries or
was due to their influence ?

Haiian-chao, one of the moet mysterious as well as one of the moet learned of the
Chinese pilgrims, waa well qualified to have been the introducer of the mystic cult of
Kuan-shi-t'ien.? Disciple of the monk Fa-shun, of whose esoteric eystem called
Fa-haing-tsung? he was the leading exponent, Haiian-chao was well known in China
as a mystic before his pilgrimage to India. At Nalanda, where he spent several
years, he was initiated into the Secret Doctrine by the venerable guru Ratnasimha,
and he is said to have attained the seventeen degrees of Yoga.

His reputation as a profound Tantric acholar reaching China, the emperor Kao-
teung sent an ambassador, Wang Haiian-te’é, to India with mission to bring him
back. Heiian-chao, thus recalled by imperial command, returned via Nepal, where
he was received in state and escorted to the Tibetan frontier.

On hin passage through ‘Tibet, he was made much of by the queen regent, who
ahowered honours upon him and, according to I-ching, gave him many presente to
take back to Chins. It is not improbable that, among these gifts, there should have
been an image of Ganeéa in his yab-yum form with possibly the Tantric texts of ritual
for his worship, since the princess Wen-ch’eng is said to have dedicated a temple to
Ganeda in Tibet. At any rate, it would seem impossible for a Chincae pilgrim to have
introduced the yab-yum form of Ganeéa without having passed through Nepal and
Tiba.

On his return to China,* Haiian-chao proceeded to Lo-yang, where he found
that the emperor Kao-teung, under the commanding influence of his favourite
concubine Wu Teét'ien, had practically given over to her the reins of govern-
ment and was, in reality, only emperor in name.’ Although a weakling, Kao-
taung was none the leas a fervent, as well as a learned Buddhist scholar. It is
said of him that he composed a preface for one of the Buddhist translations of

Heiian-teang;’ but like all of the Tang emperors, ho waa attracted by the occult

Unfortunately there are no records indicating that the secret worship of the

Such being the Tang court at the end of the seventh century and the beginning of

worship of Ganega. Hatian-chao, however, would have had barely a year to establish
the cult, for in 685 he was commanded by the emperor to proceed to Kashmir with

magic prayers or vidyd-mantras. Hsiian-chao was never to return to China, for he
died in India on his way to Kashmir.

half of the eighth century he was worshipped in his double form ; and if the introduc-

a celebrated Indian pandit Subhakara-simha.
Subhakara-simha* waa the von of a king of Orissa, one of the Tantric centres of

Unfortunately, Subbakara left no accounts of his travels as did Hsiian-tsang and
T-ching ; but there are records which note that he left India for China about AD. 715
carrying with him many Tantric texts and the Mahd-Vatrocana-sitra. On account
of the troubled state of central Asia, the only safe way of travel was by sea, and he
probably embarked for China from the port of Palur near the Black Pagoda, a
celebrated land-mark for navigators along the east coast of India.

Subhakara arrived in China in 716 and proceeded to the Chinese capital of
Ch’ang-an, where he was received at court with many honours by the greatest of
Tang emperors, Haiian-tsung. In spite of his advanced age of eighty, his activity
was remarkable, not only aa a propagandist, but as a translator; and among his
most important translations into Chinese was said to be the Mahd-Vairocana-sitra,!

Strangely enough, one of the last translations made by Subhakara when nearing
the end of his life? was a ritual for the worship of Kuan-shi-vien.? Was this Tantric
text among the works that he had brought with him from India, or was it from the
archives of oue of the vihdras at Ch’ang-an, or found among the sanskrit books left
by Hsiian-chao in the Ching-ai-ssii temple, at Lo-yang? No records solve these
questions nor why Subhakara, with so many important Buddhist works still untrans-
lated, should have spent the ehort time left him for productive activity in trans-
lating this seemingly unimportant ritual. Was the translation made by imperial
command ? Even if so, the only logical reaaon for translating the liturgic text for the
worship of Kuan-shi-t’ien into Chinese would scem to have been that the double form
of Ganeéa was already known in China and that there was need of a ritual
in order definitely to establish the cult.

At any rate, we know that towards the end of the eighth century the esoteric cult of
Kuan-shi-t’ien was already being practised in China, for it is recorded in a religious
work written by the Chinese monk Chung-shé about a.v. 7744 that Han-kuang, also
@ Buddhist monk, transmitted to the author by word of mouth a minute description
of how the double form of Ganeéa should be represented and the benefits to be
derived therefrom by worshipping him when accompanied by the prescribed rites
and ceremonies.

About this time, that is to say, in the second half of the eighth century, lived the
most remarkable of all the Chinese mystics, Hui-kuo.> At the age of seven, he had
already begun his religious studies under the guidance of the learned Sinhalese pandit
Amoghavajra, who taught him the use of mantras and dharanis; and was initiated
by him into the doctrine of yoga, mastering, before he was thirty, the mystic
Mandala of the Two Parts in which, aa we have stated above, Ganeéa figures in the
assemblies of deities. It is eaid of Hui-kuo that he not only studied every esoteric
form of Buddhist doctrine that reached China but mastered it with perfect under-

standing. May it not be inferred from this, that the esoteric cult of Kuan-shi-t'ien
was not unknown tohim? At any rate, according to tradition, his illustrious disciple,

the translation into Chinese of all similar texte. The emperor very likely prohibited,
at the same time, the making of images of Kuan-shi-t'ien, and thie is undoubtedly
the reason why no representations of the double form have heen found in China.

There is therefore a wide gap between the Vinayaka form found in the rock-cut
temple of Kung-hsien executed in the first half of the aixth century, and the Vina-
yaka form which, towards the end of the eighth or the beginning of the ninth cen-
tury, appeared in China in the mystic diagrama of the Mandala of the Two Parts,
that is, the Vajra-dhatu and the Garbha-dhatu.

‘The esoteric cult of Vairocana as taught by Amoghavajra in China in the cighth
century, which later bad an influence on the worship of Ganeéa in Japan, was founded
on the doctrine of this double mandala which had both an exoteric and an esoteric
aspect—the two sides of Absolute Reality.

According to the exoteric explanation of the diagrams, the Garbha-dhatu repre-
sented the material, the phenomenal world, and the Vajra-dhatu, the spiritual, the
noumenal world ; and although they were apparently separate, they were in reality
One and the Same or the Dharma-dhatu, that is, the Universe, the Coamic world.

According to the esoteric doctrine, Vairocana aa represented in the Vajra-dhatu
diagram was the soul of the Universe while his manifestation in the world of pheno-
mena or Vajrasattva, as figured in the centre of the Garbha-dhatu, was the embryo
of the Universe. It was taught that the Two Parte of the Coamos were symbolized
by these two mystic diagrams, the hidden meaning of which waa only to be revealed
after the disciple had received the abhigeka or ‘baptism’ of initiation.

The mandala of the Garbha-dhatu was probably introduced into China by
Subhakara-aimha. At any rate, the earliest known diagram of the mandala is
attributed to the learned pandit. In the earlicst conception of the Garbha-dhatu
diagram, the deities were not figured but were indicated by the inscription of their
names in the enclosures. It is not known what Buddhist texta were used by Subha-
kara to construct the diagram, but since the name that he inscribed in the centre

of the eight-petalled lotus is Vairocana (in reality Vajrasattva, according to esoteric
teachings) it is safe to say that it was inspired by texts bearing on the esoteric
cult of the deity. .

The diagram underwent several changes during the lifetime of Subhakara-simha.

CHAPTER VIII
GANESA IN JAPAN
HE ‘Remover of Obstacles’ was unknown in Japan up to the ninth century,
Thre the Buddhist monk, Kobd Daishi,’ who was sojourning in the Kamadera
temple at Takaishi, came upon a roll of manuscripts that proved to be a Chinese
translation of the MaAd-Vairocana-sitra,? the ‘Tantric doctrine of which, a few years
later, was to influence the rites and ceremonies of a Ganeéa cult.

As K6bd Daishi was unable to grasp the hidden meaning of the sdra, and as no
one in Japan waa able to reveal it to him, he begged permission of the Emperor
to be allowed to go to China. Accordingly, in company with the Buddhist monk,
Dengyé Daishi, he joined the suite of the ambassador to the T’ang court, and
arriving in China,? be wandered from temple to temple in search of a Buddhist
echolar versed in the Hidden Doctrine. In the Taing-long-ssii,* he found the great
pandit Hui-kuo,’ head of the Shén-yven* sect, who greeted him with the words:
‘I knew that you would come to me and have been waiting for you. Prepare to
receive the Doctrine of Mia-kué.’7

According to Shingon tradition, Hui-kué revealed the Hidden Doctrine to K5bd
Daishi after having given him the abdhiseke or * baptism’ of initiation ; and ordering a
copy of the Mandala of the Two Parts to be painted by the celebrated Chinese
painter Li-chén, he transmitted the two diagrams to his illustrious disciple. The
enclosures in the diagrams as well as the images of the deities were outlined in gold
on a black background, and the two mandalas, called the ‘Takao Mandala’, are
now in the Jing6-ji at Takaosan. The ‘T6ji Mandala’ in the Kongo-in at ‘Toji is in
colours and is believed to have been painted by Kdbé Daishi himself, in 821, from
the ‘Takao Mandala’.

In spite of the various copies of the Mandala of the Two Parts, the representations
of the deities have preserved their Indian aspect; and the forma of the four images
of Ganega as Guardian of the four sides of the central assembly or parisad (joshin-e)
of the Vajra-dhatu may be found in India. The guardian of the west,* called Vajra-
v&sin, carried a bow and arrow; the guardian of the east, Vajra-cinna, held a
parasol; Vajra-bhakaana, guardian of the south, carried a garland (mala) of flowers,
while Vajra-mukha, guardian of the north, grasped a sword. Qne of the guardians
may have the head of a wild boar possibly in order to frighten away evil epirita,
for, as will be seen below, Ganeéa was believed to have the faculty of taking on
any form he desired.

None of the representations of Ganega in the Vajra-dhatu, however, were adopted
popularly in Japan except the fifth form or Vindyaka, who was to be found directly
below Vajra-mukha, the guardian of the north side of the mandala. As the image
of Vinayaka in the Garbha-dhatu was also placed on the north side of the diagram,'
it seems to prove, especially aa they both carry the same attribute, the radish,?
that they were meant to represent the same aspect of the deity, the ‘Remover of
Obstacles’.

We have aeen above that the radish as an attribute of Ganeéa was unknown in
Ceylon as well aa in India, except in the north-east part bordering on Tibet and
Nepal, where, on the contrary, it was found almost without exception in the place of
the broken tusk.? It seems evident, therefore, that the original diagram was con-
ceived and constructed in north-east India; but by whom? According to Japanese
tradition, the sramana Nagabodhi who had taught Amoghavajra on the latter's retum
to India the method of constructing the mandalas, had received instruction in tum
from the eaint Nagarjuna who lived in the middle of the seventh century. Although
a native of southern India, ‘Tantric’ Nagarjuna, it is recorded, ‘went to the Hima-
layas, where he began to study, teach, and propagate the Mahayana’.* He was one
of the earliest writers on Tantric Buddhism; and, according to B. Bhattacharyya,
was responsible for two sddhanas in the Sddhanamdid of which one, relating to the
worship of Ekajata, he is said to have ‘rescued from the country of Bhota, which
is identified with Tibet’. It would acem from this that Nagarjuna reached at leaat
the borders of Tibet, in which case he must have been familiar with the form of
Ganesa holding the radish; and if the diagram of the Vajra-dhitu transinitted to
Amoghavajra by Nagabodhi was constructed by Nagarjuna, his guru, the presence
in the mandala of the form of Ganeéa with the radish is apparently explained, At
any rate, the representation of Ganega holding the radish seems to have been
handed down without change in its original form to Amoghavajra, who carried it to
China, whence it was introduced into Japan by Kobo Daishi.

Japanese Buddhism wove a legend around this form which might well be called
a ‘composite’ legend, for it was made up of elements that we have met with before
in other Asiatic countries, but in legends in no way concerned with Ganeéa.® The
myth runs as follows: there waa once a king by the name of Mararuratsu (2) who
ate only meat and radishes; but when he had eaten all the animals in the kingdom,
he was given the flesh of his dead subjects to cat. When there were no more dead
in the kingdom, his frightened officials killed his subjects to furnish him with food;
but the people revolted and the king, who, in reality, was a wicked demon, took

In order to understand the strange cult that developed around the double form
of Ganega, we must return to China and trace the religious movement before Kabé
Daishi’s sojourn in that country.

‘The tenets of the Hidden Doctrine (mikky6) were brought to China from India
in 720 by the Indian $ramana Vajrabodhi? and hia Sinhalese disciple, Amoghavajra.>
They introduced not only the use of mantras, dhdranis, mandalas, and other Tantric
practices, but the cult of the Adi-Buddha, Maha-Vairocana, which was founded on the
doctrine of Yoga conceived as the Union of the Individual with the Universal Spirit.

After the death of Vajrabodhi, Amoghavajra returned to Ceylon and India in
search of a copy of the Mahd-Vairocana-sitra, their manuscript of which had been
lost at sea in a violent storm on their voyage to China. We have seen above that
he met the saint Nagabodhi in south India and studied with him the mystic diagram

of the Vajra-dhatu. On returning to China,’ he brought back with him not only
the Mandala, many Tautric texts, and a copy of the Maha-Vairocana-siitra, but,
according to Japanese tradition, a statue of the Eleven-headed Avealokitesvara.?

The doctrine of Yoga which had permeated into China even before Huiian-tsang’s
time and, by his disciple Déshd, had penetrated into Japan, was still more
firmly established in China by Amoghavajra and his Chinese disciple Hui-kud, both
of whom claimed supernatural powers. When the abhiseka of Kobé Daishi and
hia initiation by Hui-ku6 took place, a miracle, according to Shén-yen (Shingon)
tradition, was performed: Kobo Daishi became One, for an instant, with the
Adi-Buddha Vairocana.*

The Shingon sect in Japan had also its tradition of a similar miracle. After his
retum to Japan,‘ Kobo Duishi was commanded to present himself at court and
disclose before the Emperor, the court officials, and the heads of all the Buddhist
schoola the tenets of the Shingon sect which he had founded. While expounding
the doctrine of mikkyé, Kobé Daishi took on, before the astonished assembly,
the aspect, for an instant, of the Adi-Buddha, who thus manifested himself in the
form of Vairocana.*

After this miracle, the dogma of the Union of the Individual with the Universal
Spirit waa firmly incorporated in the tenets of the Shingon eect; and the eccret
cult of Kangi-ten, the material demonstration of the dogma, was adopted by the sect
as the ritual for his worship which had been brought from China by Kobo Daishi.*
There are no records, however, to show whether Kobd Waishi actually carried
back with him an image of the Kangi-ten along with the Tantric texte; but if not,
since among the manuscripts brought by him to Japan was the work compiled by
the Chinese monk Chung-shé,’ it is safe to say that the Shingon Buddhists faithfully
followed its detailed prescriptions for making a statue of Kangi-ten. ‘That is, the
image ehould be executed in metal and never higher than 22 inches. The two
standing figures should be represented facing cach other with the elephant-head
posed on the left shoulder of the opposite figure and the arms of each encircling the
other. They should wear long robes falling to the feet.

There were certain characteristica of the Japanese images of Kangi-ten which
may not have figured in the work of Chung-shé. For example, in the earliest
representations, the female figure usually has the mouth open while the male deity
has the mouth closed. ‘hia detail is apparently of mystic significance in Japan,
since the Ni-d, guarding the outer gates of Buddhist temples, have the same charac-
teristic.” In fact, there is a similarity ao marked between the Ni-6 and the Kangi-ten

The ‘intermediary’ form was looked upon as representing Vindyaka and the
Eleven-headed Avalokiteévara (Kannon) ;? and the coupling of a demi-god with the
greatest of Bodhisattvas secma at first incomprehensible. Undoubtedly the earliest
interpretation of the double form in China was that of Vindyaka and his éakti,
eapecially if the form came originally from Tibet; and the identification of Avaloki-

In the Sinhalese work on iconography, the Sariputra, where the deities are
minutely described, the Sinhalese form of Avalokitesvara called *Loka-natha’ is
vhown as having cight aspects or manifestations. The first four are: Brahma-natha,
Vignu-natha, Siva-natha, Gauri-natha.>

It will be remembered that Gauri (Parvati) was the dakti par excellence of Siva.
The Bodhisattva, in his fourth and feminine aspect, thus became in his manifesta-
tion of Gauri the dakti of Siva. De La Vallée Poussin statea‘ that the personification
of the Cosmic Female Energy (Gauri) in India shows that the Chinese transformation
of Avalokiteivara into a female deity (Kwan-yin) had probably already been
effected in India.

The cighth aspect of Loka-natha mentioned in the Sariputra was Gana-natha,®
the description of whom practically tallies with that of the Indian god Ganeéa.
Thus the Bodhisattva was identified with Vindyaka also in the Sinhalese work
which was not of great antiquity, but which ia believed to have been compiled from
and based on ancient tradition. At any rate, in the Adranda-cyiha, with which
Amoghavajra was undoubtedly familiar, we find the Bodhisattva identifying himself
with Ganeéga when he declares that he will not enter into Sanyala until all human
beings are saved; and that, in order to teach the Dharma to all creatures, he will
take on the aspects of Brahma, of Visnu, of Siva, of Ganesa. .. .*

wicked king by the name of Kangi who had plunged his kingdom intoa state of
misery such that his ministers and people revolted and addressed their prayers to
the Eleven-headed Kannon to save them. The Bodhisattva accordingly took on the
aspect of a beautiful woman; and appearing before the king, excited his passion.
She, however, reaisted his advances, telling him that she was a fervent Buddhist,
and that if he desired her, he must be converted to Buddhism. The Viniyaka king
forthwith consented to follow the precepts of the Buddha and promised to protect
the Dharma, whereupon the Eleven-headed Kannon became the wife of the Vindyaka
king, Kangi. There is a variation in the legend where Senayaka, mentioned above
as son of Siva, but being in reality, Kannon, takes on a feminine form and becomes
the wife of her brother, Vinayaka, in order to turn him from his evil ways. The
interpretation of Kangi-ten as representing Kannon and Vinayaka may poasibly
be based on one of these two legends.

In the third and eaoteric aspect of Kangi-ten, the ‘ Divine Couple’ was worshipped
exclusively by the Shingon priests who were fully initiated in the mikkyd; and in
this form Kangi-ten was looked upon as pereonifying Maha-Vairocana (Universal
Spirit) and the Eleven-headed Kannon (Cosmic Female Energy). There are no
tecords to show whether this esoteric interpretation of the Kanyi-ten came originally
from China or was evolved in Japan by the Shingon sect. We only know, as we
have said above, that Kobé Daishi introduced the double form into Japan and
brought the rituals for their worship from China.

Tf, aa is believed, the statue of the Eleven-headed Avalokiteévara, now in the
Baied temple at Akasaka, was brought originally from Ceylon by Amoghavajra,!
it is probable that the learned Sinhalese pandit was responsible for the Chinese
feminine form of the Bodhisattva worshipped under the name of Kwan-yin. The
doctrine of Yoga being at the height of its popularity in China towards the end of the
eighth century, it should not be aurprising to find the double form of Ganeda taking
on @ mystic and esoteric significance; and it is conceivable that the Kuan-shi-vien
should have been looked upon in China at that epoch as personifying the Gaurt
aspect of Avalokitesvara (Coamic Sakti) and the Vairocana aspect of the Adi-
Buddha (Soul of the Universe).

Although the worship of Kangi-ten waa not held publicly in the temples, the
double form of Ganedga, in time, became known to the uninitiated as well as to the
Shingon monks and priests; but in the Hidden Doctrine of Shingon, a myatic form
of Kangi-ten was worshipped which was only revealed to the few who had attained
the highest degree of Yuga, that is, profound meditation or samadhi. The esoteric
aspect was to be conceived as representing the fusion of the double form into one,
in other words, as symbolizing supreme Yoga ; and for that reason was to be visualized
as having one body with four arms and four legs.

Thus we find in Japan another esoteric form of Ganesa, and an aspect of
Kangi-ten etill more mysterious; but here the questions arise as to where and by
whom this form was conceived. Strangely enough, while in Japan no image of this

mikkyé conception of Ganeéa is known, in China there seems to be a representa-
tion of this myatic form in a fresco which is in the Buddhist grotto temple of Tun-
huang.!

At first glance the freaco is apparently that of the usual Vindyaka, seated and
having two arma and two legs. He is represented hare to the waist and wearing
very full trousers gathered into a narrow belt. He is in the mahdrdjalild position,”
with the left knee lifted while the right leg ia bent parallel with the body.

Has this figure of Ganeéa at ‘Tun-huang four legs?

If we closely follow the folds of the loose trousers, the form of a second pair of
legs is seemingly indicated. The right knee, which would be directly under the
normal knee, is somewhat hidden by the arm of the guardian deity below; but we
can easily follow the lower line of the leg bent inward, with apparently a fold of
the trousera lying on the support of the deity. This detail will be found again at
Endere. The line of the left. knee is visible directly behind the foot of the uplifted
normal leg; and the presence of this second leg seems to be further indicated if we
follow the folds of the trousers drawn towards the left side. The two feet of the
eecond pair of legs would be naturally placed behind the normal right foot and thus
hidden underneath the folds of the voluminous trousers,

Again, in the Buddhist temple of Endere in Chinese Turkestan where a painted
panel of Ganeéa of the eighth century was found by Sir Aurel Stein,® there is
apparently represented the same mystic aspect of the Elephant-faced god that is
still worshipped in Japan. The deity is imaged with one head, four arms and,
according to Sir Aurel Stein, with two legs and ‘seated on a cushion’.

But is the Ganega of Endere seated on a cushion ?

In the frescos of the eighth century in Chinese Turkestan it was not unusual to
represent the deities seated on a cushion-dsana inatead of on a lotus flower, especially
at Dandan Uilig, where, for example, a painted panel of Vajrapani so represented
was found by Sir Aurel Stein. Were it not for the four arms, the deity might be
mistaken for a Saasanian cavalier with his black beard, tiara-ahaped turban, tight-
fitting tunic, and high boote; and he is seated on the typical cushion found in the
frescoa of Dandan Cilig which is high and stiff with rounded corners.‘

The painted panel of Ganega of Endere, on the contrary, is Indian in etyle with
no trace of Persian influence. The ‘cushion’ on which, according to Sir Aurel Stein,
he is acated, is lower than, and quite different from, the cushion-dsana of the
deitiea at Dandan Uilig.

On close and careful atudy of the original painting now in the British Museum,
it becomes apparent that the ‘cushion’, in reality, is a second pair of legs. Under
the right normal leg, for instance, the bend in the knee of the lower right leg is as
clearly indicated aa is the bend in the upper and normal knee. If we follow the
lower line of the same leg, it is apparent that it bends inward and that the foot is
behind the point of the tiger-skin, the curve alone of the dhoti indicating its presence.
The lower legs are covered with looser trousers than the normal pair, for underneath

the line of the second pair of lega are folds such as we have seen above in the frescos
of Tun-huang.

A detail which seems to indicate without question a second pair of legs is that
there is a second dhoti, red in colour, which separates the normal! legs from the
lower pair and falls in a point under the tiger-skin. The artiat has thus avoided
the difficulty of representing two pairs of feet, one above the other, by placing the
lower pair, as at Tun-huang, under the folds of the trousers, and at Endere, under
the points of the tiger-skin and red dhoti.

Does the fresco at Tun-huang, a3 well as the painted panel of Endere, represent
the double mystic aspect of Ganesa known in Japan as Kangi-ten?

If they are to be looked upon as representing the yoga, that is, the fusion of two
deities, their conception might be traced to one of the Tantric centres of India where
representations of coalesced Hindu gods may be found among the most ancient
Indian sculptures. The Hindu conception, however, aa we have seen above, waa ex-
pressed somewhat differently. The ardhanari or coaleeced form of Siva and Parvati,
for example, waa also represented by one body but with two legs and two arma, the
right side being Siva and the left side, Parvati) We bave seen above that the fusion
of four Vindyakaa into one deity was referred to in the Smrt of Yajiavalkya, but
there is no indication as to how the god was to be represented. On the other hand, in
a Tantric ritual translated from the Sanskrit by Fa-shien of the Sung dynaaty,?
there is the description of a Vindyaka with siz legs, but it is unfortunately
not explained as to whether or not, this form represented the fusion of three
deities. The author, however, understands that there existed such a conception
in Nepal.

Even if the two paintings are not to be traced to India, may not the Endere
aspect of Ganega with four legs be due to Tibetan influence? In the eighth
century the Tibetan armies crossed the bordera of China, overran Nepal and
Mongolia, and established themselves in Chinese Turkestan, where their influence
was predominant until the middle of the ninth century. Undoubtedly they brought
with them the distorted form of Buddhism as taught by the guru Padmasambhava
and carried with them the images of their deities with many arma and legs. It
should, therefore, not seem strange to find a representation of Ganeda with four
legs in Chinese Turkestan or, as a matter of fact, farther cast, since in Shingon
mikkyé, founded by the Chinese Buddhist sect, Shén-yen, there still remain vestiges
of Tibetan influence in their rites and ceremonics.*

In fact, these and other aspects of Ganesa, as we have seen above, are still
shrouded in mystery, and more than one hypothesis advanced by the author is open
to discussion. No divinity, either Hindu or Buddhist, has had go varied a ‘career’.
From a humble village deity worshipped in connexion with the harvest, he rose to
unbelicvable heighta, surpassing the Trimtrti: Brahma, Visnu and Siva; in fact,
equal to Brahman himself. Accompanying the traders as ‘Remover of Obstacles’

on their adventurous voyages across the acas, wo find him adopted by Hindu and

His cult persiated and penetrated into China, where it took on a mystic and esoteric

him secret rites and ceremonies; and although he was only a demi-god, the Elephant-

There are those who look upon Ganeda as a fétiche, as & ‘grotesque invention’.
The author, in reply, can but quote the gentle reproach of Parmentier when review-

eastern mind, seems proved when a writer looks upon the representation of the
Elephant-faced god with amusement rather than with comprehension. ... The
heavy, corpulent body required to support the massive head of an elephant, may,
on the contrary, awaken an impression of real majeaty’. If there is an element in
the representation of Ganeda which, to western eyes, ecems amusing, it is tinvolun-
tary on the part of the sculptor, and takes away nothing from the special idealiam
of the figure of the Elephant-faced Deity’.

GLOSSARY

Abhaya-mudrd. Geature usually of the right hand, indicating protection. The hand is raised
with the palm turned outward and all the fingers extended upward.

Asana. Seat of a god or of a group of gods. The padmdsana is the lotus support on which all
the important Buddhist deities are represented seated or standing. The vajrdacna or
‘diamond throne’ was the support of the Buddha when meditating under the bodhi-tree.

Asuras. Demons, enemies of the déras.

Batasa. v. Modaka.

Cintdmans, Magic gem which, in Mahdyina Buddhism, hes an esoteric significance. It is repre-
sented round, except on top where it is slightly pointed.

Dharani. Magical prayer or merely a suite of mystic eyllables supposed to be efficacious in
bringing about the desires of the worshipper.

Dhoti. Picco of stuff wound around the hips and legs and, with the exception of Indis, held at
the waist by a belt. In India it may fall to the knoes or to the ankles, but the sampot in
Indo-China and the sarony in the Malay Archipelago usually fall to the ankles.

Dhyana. Meditation. The dhydna-mudré ia represented by the hands lying on the lap with
the right above the left. Tho palms ahould be turned upward with all the fingers extended.

@rahas. Planets. Group of nine planets called the Navagrahas.

Kalada. Water-veasel.

Kapala. Bow] made out of s human skull. It in used in Tantric ceremonies.

Koranda-mukufa. v. Muluja.

Kirijo-mukula. v. Mukuja.

Tinga. Phallic symbol.

Mahdrajalild. Attitude called ‘royal ease’. The deity is seated with the right knee lifted while
the left lies on the dsana parallel with the body.

Manas. The mind when considered as a ressoning factor.

Mandala. Magic circle or diagram in which deities and symbols are placed, the whcle repre-
senting a hidden meaning.

Mantra. Short magic formule.

Modaisa. Round cake made of rice and sugar. It is somewhat larger than the balesas which
are presented in a bowl, while the modaka, being a aymbol as well as an attribute, is
presented by itself.

Mukuja. Stiff head-dress almost entirely covering the hair worn by hoth Hindu and Buddhist
Geities in India and Indo-China. The muduta may be karanda, kirita, or jaja. ‘The karanda-
mukuja is bow!-shaped and not 60 high es the other two. It msy be terraced and, in later
examples, highly ornate. The kirifa-mukwa is conical-chaped and covered with jewelled
disks and bands, while the jafé-mukwa, which is still higher, is the most complicated of
the three. It is made up of five braids of matted hair {jafas) tied into knots, coiled into
loops, and ornamented with crescent moons, cobras, &c.

Padmasana. v. Asana.

Tarjani-mudré. Threatening gesture of the hand. AU the fingers are closed except the index
which is extended.

Pajd. Hindu religious ceremony accompanied by the offering of flowers and fruits and the
sapersion of the image of the deity.

Purdsa. Religious poem wherein are found the geneslogies of the Hindu gods.
Sadhana. Magic formula for the invocation of a deity.
Séita. Sakte sect which worships Durgi as the Salts of Siva.

--- Page 118 ---
GLOSSARY 95

Sakti. Every impo-tant deity has a divine dakti looked upon as the personification of his energy.

Sramana. Learned religious teacher.

Stotra. Religious hymn to a deity.

Tilaka. Merk on the forehead indicating the religious sect to which the worshipper belongs.

Ornd. Small, round protubersnce on the furebead above the bridge of the nose. A Buddhist
mark indicating spiritual insight.

Véhano. Mount of a deity on which he may be seated or standing, or the oahana may be
represented beside him.

Vajra. Literally ‘diamond’ but usually translated ‘thunderbolt’, or that which destroys but.
in iteelf indestructible. It is represented by a short bar with prongs at each end which
touch at the tips, and is held by Hindu and Buddhist deities.

Vajrdsana. v. Asana.

Vihdra. Buddhist monastery.

Vij. Literally ‘seed’. It ia the germ of a mantra and every important deity has his vija which
is used when he is invoked.

Vitarka-mudra. Gesture of argument. The hand is raised with the palm turned outward and
all the fingera extended except the thumb and index which touch at the tips.

Zuchi. Small Japanese shrine for a deity having doors by which it may be closed.
